    In order to prove Lemma 4.3 we need several observations about how inserting additional edges
into k affects its susceptibility to planarization. First we show that under some conditions inserting


                                                    19
an edge into a subgraph of k makes it non-planar. It is an adaptation of an argument from
[CFK+ 15, §7.8].

Lemma 4.5. Let H be an extension of k and let U ⊆ [k] × [k] induce a connected subgraph of k .
Suppose that there exist distinct x, y ∈ U such that x is internal, T [x] ⊆ U , and xy ∈ E(H) \ E(k ).
Then H[U ] is not planar.

Proof. We will construct a K5 -minor within H[U ]. Let x = (i, j). The first branch set B1 is
just {x}. Next, we divide the neighborhood of x in k into sets B2 = {(i, j − 1), (i + 1, j − 1)},
B3 = {(i + 1, j), (i, j + 1)}, B4 = {(i − 1, j), (i − 1, j + 1)}. Each Bi for i ∈ [4] induces a connected
subgraph of k and each pair of them is connected by an edge from E(k ). Let B1,2,3,4 = 4i=1 Bi
                                                                                                     S
and B50 = T [x] \ B1,2,3,4 . Then B50 is adjacent in k to B2 , B3 , B4 , but not to B1 . Since xy 6∈ E(k ),
the vertex y is not contained in B1,2,3,4 . Furthermore, B50 is an unrestricted (y, B1,2,3,4 )-separator
in k . By assumption k [U ] is connected, so there exists a path P within U connecting y to B50 ,
which is disjoint from B1,2,3,4 . This path can be a singleton when y ∈ B50 . We set B5 = B50 ∪ V (P ).
Then EH (B1 , B5 ) 6= ∅ because xy ∈ E(H). Therefore B1 , B2 , B3 , B4 , B5 form branch sets of a minor
model of K5 in H[U ].

Lemma 4.6. Let S ⊆ [k] × [k] for some k ∈ N. Then the graph k − S has at most |S| + 1 connected
components.

Proof. If follows from the definition that k admits a Hamiltonian path; let P denote such a path.
Removing |S| vertices can divide the path P into at most |S| + 1 connected components. The graph
P − S can be turned into k − S by adding edges and this operation can only decrease the number
of connected components, which implies the claim.

    Next, we show that if some branch set of a contraction model of an extension of some ` contains
a connected subgraph C with edges to sufficiently many other branch sets, which are represented by
internal vertices from the grid, then any solution of size at most k must intersect C. This will imply
a criterion for the necessity of a vertex.

Lemma 4.7. Let k, λ ∈ N, H be an extension of λ , and Π be a contraction model of H in G.
Furthermore, let S ⊆ V (G) be of size at most k. Suppose there exists x ∈ V (H) and a vertex set
C ⊆ Π(x) such that G[C] is connected, C ∩ S = ∅ and there are ` = 30(k + 2) distinct internal
vertices x1 , . . . , x` from V (H) such that xi 6= x and NG (C) ∩ Π(xi ) 6= ∅ for each i ∈ [`]. Then G − S
is not planar.

Proof. Let HS ⊆ [λ] × [λ] be the vertices that contain a vertex from S in their branch sets, together
with x. Clearly |HS | ≤ k + 1, where the additive term accounts for x. Let IS = {y ∈ Int(λ) |
T [y] ∩ HS =6 ∅}. Since any vertex z ∈ [λ] × [λ] can belong to at most 25 different sets T [y], we
have |IS | ≤ 25 · (k + 1). Let X = {x1 , . . . , x` } \ IS . We have |X| ≥ 5(k + 2). By Lemma 4.6, the
graph λ − HS has at most k + 2 connected components, so there is a set U ⊆ [λ] × [λ] inducing
a connected component of λ − HS which contains at least 5 vertices from X. Note that x ∈            /U
since x ∈ HS . Since the graph λ does not contain K5 as a subgraph, there exists distinct vertices
x1 , x2 ∈ X ∩ U such that x1 x2 6∈ E(λ ). Note that T [x1 ], T [x2 ] ⊆ U by definition of U and IS . By
assumption, there exist vertices v1 ∈ Π(x1 ), v2 ∈ Π(x2 ), contained in NG (C). The last argument is
based on contracting C into x1 , which creates the edge x1 x2 if it does not already exist. Let us
consider a graph H 0 obtained from H by inserting the edge x1 x2 (if this edge is present in H, then
H 0 = H). Since C ∩ S = ∅, x 6∈ U , and C ⊆ Π(x), the graph H 0 [U ] is a minor of G − S. From
Lemma 4.5 we get that H 0 [U ] is not planar and the same holds for G − S.


                                                     20
    We now use the lemma above to argue that if some branch set Π(x) of a contraction model of an
extension of some ` contains a star-like structure of paths leading to neighbors in Π(x) of vertices
in different branch sets, the central vertex of this star is k-necessary.

Lemma 4.8. Let k, λ ∈ N, H be an extension of λ , and Π be a contraction model of H in G.
Suppose that x ∈ V (H), v ∈ Π(x), and there exists a family of ` = 31(k + 2) paths P1 , P2 , . . . , P`
in G, such that

   1. for each i ∈ [`], Pi is a (v, vi )-path, where vi ∈ Π(xi ) for some internal xi 6= x, and all interior
      vertices of Pi are contained in Π(x),

   2. for each i 6= j ∈ [`] it holds that xi 6= xj and V (Pi ) ∩ V (Pj ) = {v}.

Then v is k-necessary.

Proof. Consider any vertex set S ⊆ V (G) \ v of size at most k. We are going to show that G − S
cannot be planar. Since the paths P1 , P2 , . . . , P` are vertex-disjoint except for v, the set S can
intersect at most k of them. Let C ⊆ Π(x) be the union of the interiors of the paths not intersecting
S, together with v. Then G[C] is connected, C ∩ S = ∅ and there are ` − k ≥ 30(k + 2) different
internal vertices x0i for which NG (C) ∩ Π(x0i ) 6= ∅. Therefore the assumptions of Lemma 4.7 are
satisfied and thus G − S is not planar.

    When a vertex in H has sufficiently many internal neighbors, then its branch set contains a tree
spanning vertices adjacent to their branch sets, which is difficult to disconnect by any solution of
size at most k. The next lemma shows that in this case either mvp(G) > k or some vertex meets
the condition of Lemma 4.8.

Lemma 4.9. Let k, λ ∈ N, ` = 2 · 312 · (k + 2)3 , H be an extension of λ , and Π be a contraction
model of H in G. Suppose that there exists distinct vertices x0 , x1 , . . . , x` in [λ] × [λ] such that
for each i > 0 vertex xi is internal and x0 xi ∈ E(H). Then either mvp(G) > k or G contains a
k-necessary vertex. Furthermore, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that either finds a k-necessary
vertex or correctly concludes that mvp(G) > k.

Proof. By assumption there is a mapping ρ : [`] → Π(x0 ) such that for i ∈ [`] we have NG (ρ(i)) ∩
Π(xi ) 6= ∅, i.e., ρ(i) is a neighbor of Π(xi ) in Π(x0 ). Let R = {ρ(1), . . . , ρ(`)}.
    Since G[Π(x)] is connected, there exists a tree T in G[Π(x)] connecting vertices from R such
that each leaf of T belongs to R. Such a tree can be computed in polynomial time as follows. We
initialize T = G[Π(x)] and while there is an edge or a vertex from Π(x) \ R whose removal maintains
connectivity of T , we remove it. While T is not a tree with leaves in R, then we can always find a
removable edge or vertex.
    Suppose there exists a vertex v ∈ R such that ρ(i) = v for at least 31(k + 2) indices i ∈ [`]. Then
v trivially satisfies the conditions of Lemma 4.8 and hence v is k-necessary. Suppose now there
exists a vertex v ∈ V (T ) such that the degree of v in T is at least 31(k + 2). Since every leaf of T
belongs to R, there at 31(k + 2) paths in G[Π(x)] starting at v and ending at distinct vertices from
R, so that these paths are vertex-disjoint except for their starting point v. Since each such path
can be extended with an edge from R to a unique branch set xi , this again meets the conditions of
Lemma 4.8, so v is k-necessary.
    Suppose none of the previous two cases hold and consider any vertex set S ⊆ V (G) \ {v} of
size at most k. Since we have |ρ−1 (u)| ≤ 31(k + 2) for each u ∈ R, there are at least ` − 31 ·
k(k + 2) ≥ 312 · k(k + 2)2 indices for which ρ(i) 6∈ S. Next, observe that removing k vertices from
a tree of maximal degree bounded by 31(k + 2) can split it into at most 31 · k(k + 2) connected

                                                    21
components. Therefore, there is a vertex set C inducing a connected component of T − S, such that
               2 ·k(k+2)2
|ρ−1 (C)| ≥ 3131·k(k+2)   > 30(k + 2). In particular this means that G[C] is connected, C ∩ S = ∅ and
NG (C) ∩ Π(xi ) 6= ∅ for at least 30(k + 2) distinct vertices xi . We take advantage of Lemma 4.7 to
infer that G − S is not planar. Since the choice of S was arbitrary, this implies that mvp(G) > k.

   We are ready to prove Lemma 4.3 (restated below) and thus finish the proof of Lemma 4.4.
When there is a vertex in H with sufficiently many internal neighbors then the proof reduces to
Lemma 4.9. Otherwise, as H  b contains many non-isolated internal vertices, removing any k of them
cannot hit all the edges in H.
                             b Therefore any potential solution of size at most k leaves out some
subgraph to which Lemma 4.5 is applicable and thus it cannot be a planar modulator.

    We proceed similarly as in the proof of Lemma 4.7. Let HS ⊆ [λ]×[λ] be the vertices that contain

                                                      22
4.2   Finding a large grid minor
In order to take advantage of Lemma 4.4, we need a way to find a large triangulated grid minor when
the input graph has large treewidth. Since the triangulated grid k is a minor of a O(k) × O(k)-grid,
for this purpose it suffices to find a large grid minor. It is known that any graph G excluding a
k × k-grid minor has treewidth bounded by k O(1) . The best known bound on the exponent at k is
9 (modulo polylogarithmic factors) [CT21]. Furthermore, there is a randomized polynomial-time
algorithm that outputs either a minor model of a k × k-grid in G or a tree decomposition of width
O(k 98 (log k)O(1) ) [CC16]. However, we would like to avoid randomization and instead we show that
in our setting we can rely on the deterministic algorithm finding a large grid minor in a planar
graph of large treewidth [GT12, FLSZ19]. For our convenience we state a version of the planar
grid theorem that already provides us with a triangulated grid (and even with a contraction model
which will come in useful in further applications). All other algorithms summoned in this section
are also deterministic.
Proposition 4.10 ([FLSZ19, Thm. 14.58]). There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a
connected planar graph G and an integer t > 0, outputs either a tree decomposition of G of width
27 · t or a contraction model of Γt in G.
    In the original statement the guarantee on the decomposition width is 27
                                                                           2 · (t + 1), but for the
sake of keeping the formulas simple, we trivially upper bound t + 1 by 2t.
    A concept more general than a grid minor is a bramble [ST93] which is, similarly as a grid
minor, an object dual to a tree decomposition. Kreutzer and Tazari [KT10] introduced the notion
of a perfect bramble which enjoys some interesting properties. First,
                                                                 √ if a graph G admits a perfect
bramble of order k, then tw(G) ≥ d k2 e − 1, and if tw(G) = Ω(k 4 log k) then G admits a perfect
bramble of order k. What is particularly interesting for us, the union of subgraphs constituting
a perfect bramble forms a subgraph of maximal degree 4. This allows us to find a subgraph of
constant degree whose treewidth is still large.
Theorem 4.11 ([KT10, Thm. 5.1(c), Cor. 5.1]). There is a constant c1 > 0 and a polynomial-time
algorithm that, given a graph G with tw(G) ≥ c1 · t7 , finds a subgraph H of G, such that the maximal
degree in H is at most 4 and tw(H) ≥ t.
     Given an n-vertex subgraph H of constant degree, we can take advantage of the approximation
algorithm for Vertex planarization by Kawarabayashi and Sidiropoulos [KS19] whose approxi-
mation factor depends on the maximal degree in H and log n, which we will later bound by k O(1) .
We state it below in a form with two possible outcomes which is convenient for our application. The
first algorithm of this kind was given by Chekuri and Sidiropoulos [CS18] but with larger exponents
in the approximation factor. If we conclude that mvp(H) > k and H is a subgraph of G, then also
mvp(G) > k and we can terminate the algorithm. Otherwise, by adjusting the constants, we can
find another (possibly large) subgraph that is planar and has large treewidth. This allows us to use
the planar grid theorem on this subgraph to find a grid minor, which is also a grid minor in the
original graph G.
Theorem 4.12 ([KS19, Cor. 1.2]). There is a constant c2 > 0 and a polynomial-time algorithm
that, given an n-vertex graph G of maximum degree ∆ and an integer k, either correctly concludes
                                                                                7
that mvp(G) > k or returns a planar modulator of size at most c2 · ∆3 · (log n) 2 · k.
    If the treewidth of G is not too large, we would like to efficiently find a tree decomposition
of moderate width. To this end, we will use the polynomial-time approximation algorithm for
treewidth by Feige et al. [FHL08].

                                                 23
Theorem 4.13 ([FHL08, Thm. 6.4]). There is a polynomial-time algorithm
                                                                   √        that, given a graph G
such that tw(G) ≤ t, outputs a tree decomposition of G of width O(t log t).

   We wrap up these ingredients into the following algorithm.

Lemma 4.14. Let d > 0 be any integer constant. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
an n-vertex graph G and integer k > 0 such that log n ≤ k log k, either outputs a tree decomposition
of G of width O(k 36 ), or outputs a minor model of λ , where λ = d · k 5 , or correctly concludes that
mvp(G) > k.

Proof. We refer to constants c1 , c2 from
                                  7          √ Theorems 4.11 and 4.12. Let d1 = 28 · d and d2 be
a constant for which (log k) 2 ≤ d2 · k holds for all k ≥ 1. We begin with Theorem 4.13 for
t = c1√· (43 · c2 · d2 + d1 )7 · k 5·7 . If tw(G) ≤ t, we obtain a tree decomposition of G of width
O(k 5·7 log k) = O(k 36 ) and we are done. Otherwise tw(G) > c1 · (c2 · 43 · d2 + d1 )7 · k 5·7 and we
can use the algorithm from Theorem 4.11 to find a subgraph H of G, such that the maximal degree
in H is ∆ ≤ 4 and tw(H) ≥ (c2 · 43 · d2 + d1 ) · k 5 . As the next step, we execute the algorithm from
Theorem 4.12 for the graph H with ∆ ≤ 4. In the first case, it concludes that mvp(H) > k, which
implies that mvp(G) > k and we are done. Otherwise it returns a planar modulator SH ⊆ V (H) of
size at most
                                         7                          7
                      c2 · ∆3 · (log n) 2 · k ≤ c2 · 43 · (k log k) 2 · k ≤ c2 · 43 · d2 · k 5 .
The graph H 0 = H − SH is planar and tw(H 0 ) ≥ tw(H) − |SH | ≥ d1 · k 5 > 27 · d · k 5 because removing
a single vertex can decrease the treewidth by at most one. For the graph H 0 we can take advantage
of the planar grid theorem. If it is disconnected, we focus on the connected component having large
treewidth. We use Proposition 4.10 with t = λ = d · k 5 and, since treewidth of H 0 is sufficiently
large, the algorithm must return a contraction model of Γλ in H 0 . Since H 0 is a subgraph of G, we
can turn it into a minor model of λ in G.

   Finally, we need to justify the assumption that log n is small. If log n is larger than k log k, then
the exponential term of the form 2O(k log k) becomes polynomial. In such a case, we can use the
known FPT algorithm for Vertex planarization.

Theorem 4.15 ([JLS14]). There is an algorithm that, given an n-vertex graph G and an integer k,
runs in time 2O(k log k) · n and either correctly concludes that mvp(G) > k or outputs a planar
modulator of size mvp(G) ≤ k.

    After a large grid minor is identified, we invoke Lemma 4.4 to find either a necessary vertex or
an irrelevant vertex. Once such a vertex is located we can remove it from the graph and proceed
recursively. In the end, we either solve the problem or arrive at the case where treewidth is bounded.

Proposition 4.16. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph G and an integer k,
either

   1. outputs a planar modulator in G of size mvp(G) ≤ k, or

   2. correctly concludes that mvp(G) > k, or

   3. outputs a graph G0 together with a tree decomposition of width O(k 36 ) and an integer k 0 ≤ k,
      such that if mvp(G) ≤ k, then mvp(G0 ) = mvp(G) − (k − k 0 ). Furthermore, there is a
      polynomial-time algorithm that, given G, k, G0 , and a planar modulator S 0 in G0 of size at
      most k 0 , outputs a planar modulator S in G such that |S| = |S 0 | + (k − k 0 ).


                                                  24
Proof. First, we check if k log k < log n. If yes, then the running time 2O(k log k) ·n from Theorem 4.15
becomes polynomial. In this is the case, we take advantage of the exact FPT algorithm to either
conclude that mvp(G) > k or find a planar modulator of size mvp(G) ≤ k. Otherwise log n ≤ k log k
and we can execute the algorithm from Lemma 4.14. We choose a sufficiently large constant d to
ensure that 312 · (2k + 7)3 + 4 ≤ d · k 5 for all k ≥ 1. If the algorithm returns a tree decomposition of
width O(k 36 ) or concludes that mvp(G) > k, we are done. Otherwise we obtain a minor model of
λ in G, where λ = 312 · (2k + 7)3 + 4 (if the returned grid is larger, we can easily truncate the
minor model). We supply the computed minor model to the algorithm from Lemma 4.4. It either
outputs a k-irrelevant or k-necessary vertex v, or concludes that mvp(G) > k. In the first case we
recurse on the instance (G − v, k), in the second case we recurse on (G − v, k − 1), and on the last
case we terminate the algorithm.
    If v is k-irrelevant then any planar deletion set of size at most k in G − v is also valid in G.
This entails that whenever mvp(G) ≤ k then mvp(G − v) = mvp(G). If v is k-necessary then
mvp(G) ≤ k implies mvp(G − v) = mvp(G) − 1 and so mvp(G − v) > k − 1 implies mvp(G) > k.
Furthermore, for any planar deletion set S in G − v, clearly S ∪ {v} is a planar deletion set in G.
    Let (G0 , k 0 ) be the instance on which the recursion has stopped. Observe that when we are given
a planar modulator S 0 in G0 of size at most k 0 then by inserting the removed necessary vertices to S 0
we can turn it into a planar modulator in G of size |S 0 | + (k − k 0 ). If the algorithm has terminated
with a planar modulator in G0 of size mvp(G0 ) ≤ k 0 then we turn it into a planar modulator in G
of size mvp(G0 ) + (k − k 0 ) = mvp(G). If we received a message that mvp(G0 ) > k 0 then it implies
that mvp(G) > k. Finally, if the algorithm terminated with a tree decomposition of G0 of width
O((k 0 )36 ) we can return it and whenever the lifting algorithm receives a planar modulator S 0 in G0
of size mvp(G0 ) ≤ k 0 , it can be turned into a planar modulator in G of size |S 0 | + (k − k 0 ).


5     Constructing a strong modulator
In this section we work with a given tree decomposition of G of width k O(1) . We are going to process
such a decomposition several times and so we begin with presenting a general procedure which scans
the tree decomposition bottom-up and detects subgraphs with certain properties. The procedure is
based on the well-known covering/packing duality for connected subgraphs of a bounded treewidth
host graph (cf. [RT17, Lemma 3.10]). While such arguments are typically applied to pack and cover
cycles or minor models, we will need it in a slightly more general form. In the lemma below, we
therefore work with a family F of vertex subsets of G which is hereditary (so that F is closed under
taking subsets), requiring only a polynomial-time subroutine to recognize whether a set belongs
to F or not.

Lemma 5.1. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph G, a tree decomposition (T, χ)
of G, an integer k, and a polynomial-time subroutine that tests membership of vertex subsets S ⊆ V (G)
in a hereditary family F ⊆ 2V (G) , returns one of the following:
                                                                                S
   1. a subset T0 ⊆ V (T ) of size at most k, such that every set C ⊆ V (G) \ t∈T0 χ(t), for which
      G[C] is connected, belongs to F, or

    2. a collection of k + 1 disjoint vertex subsets (Vi )k+1
                                                          i=1 , such that each G[Vi ] is connected and
       Vi 6∈ F.

Proof. Recall that notation Tt stands for the subtree of T rooted at t ∈ V (T ). Observe that for
a given set U ⊆ V (G), we can check if all its connected subsets belong to F by checking if each
connected component of G[U ] belongs to F.

                                                   25
     We consider an iterative process: in each step we identify a node t ∈ V (T ) whose bag separates
some connected subsets which belong to F from the rest of the graph but their union together with
the bag of t contains a connected subset which does not belong to F.
     Initialize S0 = ∅ and U0 = ∅. In the i-th step, i ≥ 1, we consider the induced subgraph G − Ui−1 .
For t ∈ V (T ), let χi (t) = χ(t) \ Ui−1 . The pair (T, χi ) is a tree decomposition of G − Ui−1 . We
choose ti ∈ V (T ) to be a deepest node (breaking ties arbitrarily) so that χi (Tti ) contains a connected
vertex set which does not belong to F – let us refer to this set as Vi . Let Ui = Ui−1 ∪ χi (Tti )
and Si = Si−1 ∪ χi (ti ). By Observation 3.5, the set χi (ti ) separates G − Ui−1 into V (G) \ Ui and
χi (Tti ) \ χi (ti ). By the choice of ti , we know that all the connected subsets of χi (Tti ) \ χi (ti ) belong
to F. We continue this process until all connected subsets of V (G) \ Ui belong to F, so there is no
way to choose ti+1 .
     If we have performed at least k + 1 iterations, we return the sets V1 , V2 , . . . , Vk+1 . Since Vi ⊆ Ui
and Vj ∩ Ui = ∅ for j > i, these sets are disjoint. Furthermore, each of them is connected and does
not belong to F.
     If we have terminated in at most ` ≤ k iterations, then the set S` is a union of ` sets χi (ti ) and
χi (ti ) ⊆ χ(ti ). Observe that V (G) \ U` and sets χi (Tti ) \ χi (ti ), i ∈ [`], are pairwise separated by S`
in G. These sets contain only connected subsets from F. In this case we return the set of nodes
{t1 , . . . , t` }.

5.1    Planarizing small neighborhoods
In this section we work towards proving Lemma 5.4, which essentially gives a reduction to problem
instances with a tidy planar modulator U such that each component of G − U remains planar even
when reinserting two arbitrary vertices from U . To get there, we need two ingredients.
    The following simple lemma uses the fact that obstructions to planarity are connected together
with covering/packing duality in a tree decomposition to either find a planar modulator of bounded
size or conclude that any planar modulator must be large.

Lemma 5.2. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph G with a tree decomposition
of width t − 1 and an integer k, either returns a planar modulator in G of size at most k · t or
correctly concludes that mvp(G) > k.

Proof. We apply Lemma 5.1 for the family F of vertex subsets which induce planar subgraphs of
G. This property is hereditary and decidable in polynomial time [HT74], so the conditions of the
lemma are satisfied. In the first scenario, the algorithm returns a set of at most k nodes, so that the
union of their bags is a planar modulator of size at most k · t. Otherwise, the algorithm returns
k + 1 vertex-disjoint non-planar subgraphs. This testifies that every planar modulator must have at
least k + 1 vertices.

    The next ingredient needed for Lemma 5.4 is the following encapsulation of Lemma 5.1.

Lemma 5.3. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph G, sets X0 ⊆ X ⊆ V (G),
such that G − X is planar, a tree decomposition of G − X of width t − 1, and integer k, returns one
of the following:

   1. a set Y ⊆ V (G) \ X of size at most k · t, such that for each vertex set C ⊆ V (G) \ (X ∪ Y ),
      for which G[C] is connected, the graph G[C ∪ X0 ] is planar, or

   2. a collection of k + 1 disjoint vertex subsets (Vi )k+1
                                                         i=1 such that Vi ⊆ V (G) \ X, G[Vi ] is connected,
      and G[Vi ∪ X0 ] is non-planar.


                                                      26
Proof. We apply Lemma 5.1 for the graph G − X and family F of its vertex subsets defined as
follows: C ∈ F if G[C ∪ X0 ] is planar. In the first case, the union of bags of the returned nodes has
size at most k · t and satisfies condition (1). Otherwise, we obtain k + 1 disjoint connected vertex
sets that do not belong to F.

    Using these ingredients we now present the lemma that reduces an instance to one with a suitably
tidy modulator, or detects a small vertex set that allows an approximation-preserving reduction to
a smaller graph.
Lemma 5.4. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph G, a set X ⊆ V (G) of
size m, such that G − X is planar, a tree decomposition of G − X of width t − 1, and an integer k,
returns one of the following:
  1. a set U ⊇ X of size at most 23 · m2 · k 3 · t3 , such that for each vertex set C ⊆ V (G) \ U ,
     for which G[C] is connected, and for each X0 ⊆ U of size at most 2, the graph G[C ∪ X0 ] is
     planar, or

  2. a set X0 ⊆ V (G) of size at most 2, such that mvp(G) ≤ k implies mvp(G − X0 ) ≤ mvp(G) − 1.
Proof. For every x ∈ X we apply Lemma 5.3 with X0 = {x} and remaining parameters as in the
statement of this lemma.
     Suppose that for at least one call we obtain answer (2): a collection of k + 1 disjoint vertex
subsets (Vi )k+1
             i=1 such that Vi ⊆ V (G) \ X and G[Vi ∪ {x}] is non-planar. Assume that G admits
a planar modulator S of size ` ≤ k. Then for some i ∈ [k + 1] it holds that Vi ∩ S = ∅. Since
G[Vi ∪ {x}] is non-planar, we have x ∈ S. Therefore S \ {x} is a planar modulator for G − {x} of
size ` − 1 and the set {x} satisfies condition (2).
     Suppose otherwise, that for each x ∈ X we computed a set Yx ⊆ V (G) \ X of size at most
k · t, such that for each vertex Sset C ⊆ V (G) \ (X ∪ Yx ), for which G[C] is connected, the graph
G[C ∪ {x}] is planar. Let Y = x∈X Yx . For each x ∈ X it holds that every connected component
of G − (X ∪ Y ) is contained in some connected component of G − (X ∪ Yx ). Therefore for each
x ∈ X and each C ⊆ V (G) \ (X ∪ Y ), for which G[C] is connected, the graph G[C ∪ {x}] is planar.
     Next, we iterate over all subsets X0 ⊆ X ∪ Y of size 2 and for each we apply Lemma 5.3 to
the tree decomposition of G − (X ∪ Y ) and sets X0 , X ∪ Y . Note that we can easily turn a tree
decomposition of G − X into a tree decomposition of G − (X ∪ Y ) without increasing the width.
     Again, suppose that for at least one call we obtain answer (2): a collection of k + 1 disjoint
vertex subsets (Vi )k+1
                     i=1 such that Vi ⊆ V (G) \ X and G[Vi ∪ X0 ] is non-planar. Assume that G
admits a planar modulator S of size ` ≤ k. Then for some i ∈ [k + 1] it holds that Vi ∩ S = ∅. Since
G[Vi ∪ X0 ] is non-planar, we have X0 ∩ S 6= ∅. Therefore S \ X0 is a planar modulator for G − X0
of size at most ` − 1 and the set X0 satisfies condition (2).
     In the remaining case, for each considered X0 = {x, y} we have obtained a set Z{x,y} ⊆
V (G) \ (X ∪ Y ) of size at most k · t, such that for each vertex set
                                                                  S C ⊆ V (G) \ (X ∪ Y ∪ Z{x,y} ), which
is connected in G, the graph G[C ∪ {x, y}] is planar. Let Z = {x,y}⊆X∪Y Z{x,y} and U = X ∪ Y ∪ Z.
For each set {x, y} ⊆ X ∪ Y it holds that every connected component of G − U is contained in
some connected component of G − (X ∪ Y ∪ Z{x,y} ). Therefore for each {x, y} ⊆ X ∪ Y and each
C ⊆ V (G) \ U , which is connected in G, the graph G[C ∪ {x, y}] is planar.
     To argue that the constructed set U satisfies condition (2), it remains to handle pairs {x, y} ⊆ U ,
which are not contained in X ∪ Y . Let C ⊆ V (G) \ U be a vertex set connected in G. If x, y ∈ Y ∪ Z,
then C ∪ {x, y} ⊆ V (G) \ X and this set induces a planar graph because G − X is planar by the
assumption. The remaining case is when x ∈ X and y ∈ Z. If y ∈ NG (C), then C ∪ {y} is a
connected subset of V (G) \ (X ∪ Y ) and it remains planar with x attached due to the first round

                                                   27
of the construction. If y 6∈ NG (C), then G[C ∪ {x}] is planar by the same argument as above and
adding a vertex which has degree 1 or is isolated preserves planarity.
    We established that attaching any pair of vertices from U to any connected component of G − U
preserves its planarity. It follows that the same holds if we consider attaching just a single vertex
from U . Finally, we estimatethe size of U . The set Y is a union of m sets Yx and so |Y | ≤ m · k · t.
The set Z is a union of X∪Y 2    ≤ (2|Y |)2 ≤ 4 · (m · k · t)2 many sets Z{x,y} and so |Z| ≤ 4 · m2 · k 3 · t3 .
As |X ∪ Y | ≤ |Z|, the upper bound follows.

5.2    Reducing the neighborhood of planar components
Using Lemma 5.4 we can reduce to an instance which has a tidy planar modulator U such that
each component of G − U remains planar when reinserting two vertices from U . In this section we
work towards obtaining even stronger properties for components outside the modulator as described
in Definition 3.4, so that each component outside the modulator induces a planar subgraph when
taken together with its entire neighborhood.
    The following lemma is inspired by existing tools to compute protrusion decompositions for
problems on planar graphs (cf. [FLSZ19, Lemma 15.13]). It is used to either build an enhanced
modulator which gives a bound on the size of the neighborhood of each remaining component,
or identify a triple of vertices that allows an approximation-preserving graph reduction step by
detecting three vertices which are part of many minor models of K3,3 .

Lemma 5.5. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph G, set X ⊆ V (G) of size m
such that G − X is planar, a tree decomposition (T, χ) of G − X of width t − 1, and an integer
k > 0, returns one of the following:

   1. a set Y ⊆ V (G) \ X of size at most 23 · m3 · k · t, such that for each connected vertex set C of
      G − (X ∪ Y ) it holds that |NG (C) ∩ X| ≤ 2 and |NG (C) ∩ Y | ≤ 2t,

   2. a triple {v1 , v2 , v3 } ⊆ X and a collection of k + 3 disjoint vertex subsets (Vi )k+1
                                                                                            i=1 such that
      Vi ⊆ V (G) \ X, G[Vi ] is connected, and E(Vi , vj ) 6= ∅ for all i ∈ [k + 3], j ∈ [3].

Proof. We apply Lemma 5.1 for the graph G − X, parameter k 0 = m3 · (k + 2) and family F of its
vertex subsets defined as follows: C ∈ F if |NG (C) ∩ X| ≤ 2.
    In the first scenario, the algorithm returns                       0
                                            S a set S of at most k nodes from V (T ), so that for
each connected component C of G − (X ∪ t∈S χ(t)) it holds that |NG (C) ∩ X| ≤ 2,     S which implies
the same statement for each connected vertex set C. Let M = LCA(S) and Y = t∈M χ(t)). By
Lemma 3.9, point (b), |M | ≤ 2|S| − 1 and so |Y | ≤ 2 · m3 · (k + 2) · t. We upper bound k + 2 with
3k ≤ 22 k to get the formulated estimation. By Lemma 3.9, point (a), for each connected vertex set
C of G − (X ∪ Y ) (which is contained in a union of bags from a connected subtree of T − M by
Observation 3.6) the set NG (C) ∩ Y is contained in at most 2 bags, hence |NG (C) ∩ Y | ≤ 2t. We
have M ⊇ S, so it also holds that |NG (C) ∩ X| ≤ 2.
    In the second scenario, we obtain a collection of m3 · (k + 2) + 1 disjoint vertex sets Vi which
induce connected subgraphs of G and do not belong to F. Each Vi contains some triple from X in
its neighborhood and, since there are m         3
                                        3 ≤ m distinct triples from X, one triple has to appear in
the common neighborhood of at least (k + 3) sets Vi . It can be identified in polynomial time by
enumerating all the triples from X.

   Using the preceding lemma, the next algorithm makes progress towards obtaining a strong
modulator by ensuring planarity for each connected component outside the modulator, even when
taken together with its neighborhood.

                                                      28
Lemma 5.6. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph G, set X ⊆ V (G) of size m
such that G − X is planar, a tree decomposition of G − X of width t − 1, and an integer k > 0,
returns one of the following:
   1. a set Z ⊇ X of size at most 213 · m6 · k 10 · t10 , such that for each connected vertex set C in
      G − Z the graph G[NG (C)] is planar and |NG (C) ∩ Z| ≤ 2t + 2, or

   2. a set X0 ⊆ V (G) of size at most 3, such that mvp(G) ≤ k implies mvp(G − X0 ) ≤ mvp(G) − 1.
In particular, when G is planar (so mvp(G) = 0) then the algorithm must return answer (1).
Proof. We apply Lemma 5.4 for the same arguments as in the statement of this lemma. We either
obtain a set X0 ⊆ V (G) of size at most 2, which satisfies condition (2) or a set U ⊇ X of size
at most 23 · m2 · k 3 · t3 , such that each connected vertex set C ⊆ V (G) \ U remains planar after
attaching any vertex or a pair of vertices from U . In the first case we are done and in the second
case, we proceed with Lemma 5.5 by substituting X ← U . We distinguish two cases, depending on
the outcome of Lemma 5.5.
    Suppose first that we have obtained a triple {v1 , v2 , v3 } ⊆ U and a collection of k + 3 disjoint
vertex subsets (Vi )k+1      i=1 such that Vi ⊆ V (G) \ U , G[Vi ] is connected, and E(Vi , vj ) 6= ∅ for all
i ∈ [k + 3], j ∈ [3]. Assume that G admits a planar modulator S of size ` ≤ k. Then for
some {i1 , i2 , i3 } ∈ [k + 3] it holds that Vij ∩ S = ∅ for j ∈ [3]. If {v1 , v2 , v3 } ∩ S = ∅, then
{v1 , v2 , v3 , Vi1 , Vi2 , Vi3 } would form branch sets of a K3,3 minor in G − S, which is not possible.
Hence, {v1 , v2 , v3 } ∩ S 6= ∅ and S \ {v1 , v2 , v3 } is a planar modulator in G − {v1 , v2 , v3 } of size at
most ` − 1. We can thus return X0 = {v1 , v2 , v3 } to satisfy condition (2).
    Now suppose that the algorithm from Lemma 5.5 terminated with scenario (1). We have obtained
a set Y of size at most 23 · |U |3 · k · t ≤ 212 · m6 · k 10 · t10 . Let Z = U ∪ Y , which satisfies the claimed
size bound since |U | ≤ |Y |. For each connected vertex set C of G − (U ∪ Y ) Lemma 5.5 guarantees
that |NG (C) ∩ U | ≤ 2 and |NG (C) ∩ Y | ≤ 2t, so |NG (C) ∩ Z| ≤ 2t + 2. The set NG [C] \ U is a
connected vertex subset of V (G) \ U and by the properties of U guaranteed by Lemma 5.4 for
X0 = NG (C) ∩ U , this implies that G[(NG [C] \ U ) ∪ (NG (C) ∩ U )] is planar, and so G[NG [C]] is
planar.

   We are ready to state and prove the main result of this subsection, which encapsulates the
previous reduction steps using the notion of strong modulators (Definition 3.4). In addition, it gives
a bound on the number of connected components with at least three neighbors which occur outside
the new modulator X 0 , using the terminology of fragmentation (Definition 3.3).
Proposition 5.7. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph G with a tree decompo-
sition of width t − 1, and an integer k > 0, either correctly concludes that mvp(G) > k, or outputs a
graph G0 with a (2t + 2)-strong planar modulator X 0 , and an integer k 0 ≤ k, so that:
   1. if mvp(G) ≤ k, then mvp(G0 ) ≤ mvp(G) − (k − k 0 ),

   2. the fragmentation of X 0 in G0 is O(k 49 t48 ),

   3. given a planar modulator S 0 in G0 , one can turn it into a planar modulator in G of size at
      most |S 0 | + 3 · (k − k 0 ) in polynomial time.
Proof. We begin with Lemma 5.2 to either find a planar modulator X of size at most λ = k · t or
conclude that mvp(G) > k. In the first case, we can easily turn the given tree decomposition of G
into a tree decomposition of G − X of no greater width. We keep the values of t, λ intact within
this proof, while k is a free variable in the recursion.

                                                      29
    We present the graph modification as a recursive process in which we are given a tuple
(G, X, T, χ, k) where X is a planar modulator in G of size at most λ and (T, χ) is a tree de-
composition of G − X of width at most t. We either conclude that (G, X, k) satisfies the claim or
recurse on some tuple (G0 , X 0 , k − 1, T 0 , χ0 ) so that |X 0 | ≤ |X|. In order to prove the forward safeness
we will show that when mvp(G) ≤ k then mvp(G0 ) ≤ mvp(G) − 1. To prove the backward safeness
we show that when given a planar modulator S 0 in G0 we can turn in into a planar modulator in G
of size at most |S 0 | + 3.
    Given a tuple (G, X, T, χ, k), we apply Lemma 5.6. If we get the outcome (2), we obtain a set
X0 ⊆ V (G) of size at most 3 such that mvp(G) ≤ k implies mvp(G − X0 ) ≤ mvp(G) − 1: this yields
the forward safeness. The backward safeness follow from the fact that when S is a planar modulator
in G − X0 then S ∪ X0 is a planar modulator in G. Since G − (X ∪ X0 ) is a subgraph of G − X, we
can turn (T, χ) into a tree decomposition of G − (X ∪ X0 ) of no greater width.
    If we get the outcome (1) from Lemma 5.6, we obtain a (2t + 2)-strong planar modulator Z ⊇ X.
We have |Z| ≤ 213 · |X|6 · k 10 · t10 ≤ 213 · k 16 · t16 . We stop the recursion at this point.
    In order to bound the fragmentation of Z, let us consider another recursive process, in which we
process a triple (G, Z, k) where Z is a (2t + 2)-strong planar modulator in G. Whenever we recurse
on (G0 , Z 0 , k − 1) we demand |Z 0 | ≤ |Z| and consider the same safeness conditions as before: (a)
mvp(G) ≤ k implies mvp(G0 ) ≤ mvp(G) − 1, and (b) when given a planar modulator S 0 in G0 we
can turn in into a planar modulator in G of size at most |S 0 | + 3.
    Given a triple (G, Z, k) we enumerate all size-3 subsets of Z and look for a subset Z0 :=
{v1 , v2 , v3 } ⊆ Z, such that for at least k + 3 connected components (Ci )k+3      i=1 in CC(G − Z) we have
Z0 ⊆ NG (Ci ). Consider any planar modulator S in G of size ` ≤ k. Analogously as in Lemma 5.3,
we observe that for some {i1 , i2 , i3 } ∈ [k + 3] it must hold that Cij ∩ S = ∅ for each j ∈ [3]. If
Z0 ∩ S = ∅, then {v1 , v2 , v3 , Ci1 , Ci2 , Ci3 } would form branch sets of a K3,3 minor in G − S, which
is not possible. Hence, Z0 ∩ S 6= ∅ and S \ Z0 is a planar modulator in G − Z0 of size at most
` − 1. In such a scenario, we recurse on (G − Z0 , Z \ Z0 , k − 1). We have mvp(G) ≤ k implies
mvp(G − Z − 0) ≤ mvp(G) − 1. Again, when S is a planar modulator in G − Z0 then S ∪ Z0 is a
planar modulator in G. Furthermore, Z \ Z0 is a (2t + 2)-strong planar modulator in G − Z0 .
    When we can no longer find such a set Z0 ⊆ Z, we stop the recursion and return G and X 0 = Z.
By a counting argument, the number of connected components in G − Z with at least 3 neighbors
cannot be larger than |Z|3 · (k + 2) = O(k 49 t48 ), which gives a bound on the fragmentation of Z.


6     Reducing the radial diameter
From now on we work with an extended modulator so that GhCi is planar for each connected
component C of G − X. We will consider a boundaried plane graph H given by a plane embedding
of GhCi; we begin with an arbitrary embedding. We can describe modifications to the graph G
by modifications to H as every vertex or edge in H represents a vertex or edge in G. To make
the arguments clear, we avoid modifying vertices from ∂(H) or edges incident to ∂(H), so no such
modification to H affects other parts of the graph G outside C.
    In order to replace H with a smaller boundaried graph H 0 , which approximately preserves its
structure, we are going to reduce the task to the case where the vertices of ∂(H) are embedded in a
convenient way. In particular, we are interested in the case there ∂(H) is located on a single face
of H. As a step in this direction, we first reduce the radial diameter of H to k O(1) . One obstacle
towards this is given by the existence of a large subset X ⊆ V (H) \ ∂(H), with a small (constant)
boundary, that separates some vertices from ∂(H) in H. We are going to compress such subsets
using the technique of protrusion replacement. This technique will also come in useful in the final


                                                      30
part of the proof.

6.1   Protrusion replacement
An r-protrusion in a graph G is a vertex set X ⊆ V (G), such that |∂G (X)| ≤ r and tw(G[X]) ≤ r.
For a vertex set X ⊆ V (G) with |∂G (X)| = r, a bijection λ : [r] → ∂G (X), and a labeled r-
boundaried graph H, the act of replacing X by H through λ means (1) creating labeled r-boundaried
graphs H X = (G[X], ∂G (X), λ), H V (G)\X = (G[(V (G) \ X) ∪ ∂G (X)], ∂G (X), λ), and (2) replacing
G = H V (G)\X ⊕ H X by G0 = H V (G)\X ⊕ H. The graph H X is introduced above only for the sake
of giving a more detailed view on the process. We say that G0 is obtained from G by replacing a set
X ⊆ V (G) if there exist H, λ so that X is replaced by H through λ.
    The standard protrusion replacement technique [BFL+ 16, FLMS12] is aimed at reducing large
r-protrusions, where r = O(1), while preserving the optimum value of the instance for the considered
optimization problem. Since we want to perform lifting of a potentially non-optimal solution, we
use the lossless variant of the technique which provides us with such a mechanism. The definition
below can be instantiated for any min-CMSO (or max-CMSO) vertex subset problem Π. In such a
problem we are given a graph G and the objective is to find a set S ⊆ V (G) minimizing |S| such that
the predicate PΠ (G, S), expressible in Counting Monadic Second-Order (CMSO) logic, is satisfied.
For our purposes it is only relevant that this definition captures the Vertex planarization
problem. We specify the labeling λ directly in the definition whereas the formulation in [FLMS20]
assumes an implicit labeling on the boundary of X.

Definition 6.1 (Lossless Protrusion Replacer [FLMS12, FLMS20]). A lossless protrusion replacer
for a min-CMSO vertex subset problem Π is a family of algorithms, with one algorithm for every
constant r. The r-th algorithm has the following specifications. There exists a constant γ(r) such that
given an instance G and an r-protrusion X in G containing at least γ(r) vertices, |∂G (X)| = r0 ≤ r,
the algorithm runs in polynomial time and outputs an instance G0 with the following properties.

   • G0 is obtained from G by replacing (G[X], ∂G (X)) by a labeled r0 -boundaried graph H with
     less than γ(r) vertices, through some labeling λ : [r0 ] → ∂G (X), and thus V (G0 ) < V (G).

   • OPT(G0 ) ≤ OPT(G).

   • There is an algorithm that runs in polynomial time and, given a feasible solution S 0 to G0 ,
     outputs a set X ∗ ⊆ X such that S = (S 0 \ V (H)) ∪ X ∗ is a feasible solution to G and
     |S| ≤ |S 0 | + OPT(G) − OPT(G0 ).

    In the original statement, the lossless protrusion replacer is defined to run in time O(|X|). This
is important if we aim at optimizing the running time of the algorithm but requires some care with
specifying the data structures for maintaining the decomposition into r-protrusion. For the sake
of keeping the presentation simple, we have chosen a weaker specification with just a polynomial
running time guarantee.

Lemma 6.2. The Vertex planarization problem admits a lossless protrusion replacer. Moreover,
the choice of the replacement H and the transposition value mvp(G) − mvp(G0 ) depends only on the
labeled boundaried graph (G[X], ∂G (X), λ).

Proof. The Vertex planarization problem is a special case of F-Minor Deletion for the
family of forbidden minors F = {K5 , K3,3 }. Fomin et al. [FLMS12, FLMS20] have shown that
every min-CMSO vertex subset problem Π that is strongly monotone admits a lossless protrusion
replacer. Without going into details, both conditions hold for F-Minor Deletion for every family

                                                  31
F composed of connected graphs [BFL+ 16, Lem. 8.4]. We remark that the proof of the strong
monotonicity property in [BFL+ 16] is formulated for the case where the family F contains at least
one planar graph, but this condition is not used in this particular proof. Finally, by examining the
proof of [FLMS20, Thm. 9] it follows that the choice of the replacement H and the transposition
value OPT(G) − OPT(G0 ) = mvp(G) − mvp(G0 ) depends only on the labeled boundaried graph
(G[X], ∂G (X), λ) and is indifferent to the structure of G − X.

    In our applications, we need to replace a vertex set X ⊆ V (G), such that G[X] is planar and
|∂G (X)| ≤ r for constant r, but the treewidth of G[X] is not necessarily bounded. It is easy to
show that for any minimum planar modulator S it holds that |S ∩ X| ≤ r, as otherwise the solution
(S \ X) ∪ ∂G (X) would be smaller. By an argument based on grid minors, if tw(G[X]) is larger
than some function of r, then X contains a vertex irrelevant for minimum solutions. As we work
with solutions which are not necessarily optimal, we need an additional argument that allows us to
assume that |S ∩ X| ≤ r.

Definition 6.3. We say that a planar modulator S in G is r-locally optimal if there are no sets
S 0 ⊆ S and S ∗ ⊆ V (G) \ S such that |S 0 | > r, |S ∗ | ≤ r, and (S \ S 0 ) ∪ S ∗ is also a planar modulator.

Lemma 6.4. Given an n-vertex graph G with a planar modulator S ⊆ V (G), and an integer r, one
can find, in time nO(r) , an r-locally optimal planar modulator Sb of size at most |S|.

Proof. Suppose that S is not r-locally optimal, so there are sets S 0 ⊆ S and S ∗ ⊆ V (G) \ S such
that |S 0 | > r, |S ∗ | ≤ r, and (S \ S 0 ) ∪ S ∗ is also a planar modulator. Let S 00 be an arbitrary subset of
S 0 of size r + 1. Then (S \ S 00 ) ∪ S ∗ is a superset of (S \ S 0 ) ∪ S ∗ , so it is also a planar modulator, and
it is smaller than S. The algorithm checks all O(nr+1 ) candidates for S 00 , and all O(rnr ) candidates
for S ∗ . If for any pair (S 00 , S ∗ ), the set (S \ S 00 ) ∪ S ∗ is also a planar modulator (this can be checked
in time O(n + m) [HT74]), we set S ← (S \ S 00 ) ∪ S ∗ and repeat the algorithm. In every step we
decrease the size of S, so after at most n steps the process must terminate. Since at this moment we
cannot find any pair (S 00 , S ∗ ) that would provide further refinement, the obtained planar modulator
must be r-locally optimal.

   Consider a set X = GhAi for some A, for which |NG (A)| ≤ r. The lifting algorithm can use
Lemma 6.4 to find a solution S satisfying |S ∩ X| ≤ r. Therefore any vertex v ∈ X which can be
surrounded by r + 3 connected (v, ∂G (X)) separators is irrelevant for S. We follow the standard
argument based on a grid contraction to find such a vertex whenever tw(G[X]) is large.

Lemma 6.5. Let G be a graph, A ⊆ V (G), and GhAi be a (≤ r)-boundaried connected planar graph.
If tw(GhAi) > 27(2r + 7)(r + 1), then there exists a vertex v ∈ A and a sequence of r + 3 connected
pairwise-disjoint (v, NG (A))-separators in GhAi. Such a vertex v can be found in polynomial time,
when given G and A. Furthermore, given G, A, v, and a planar modulator S in G − v, one can find,
in time nO(r) , a planar modulator in G of size at most |S|.

Proof. We apply Proposition 4.10 for t = (2r + 7)(r + 1). As the treewidth of GhAi is larger than
27 · t, we can find, in polynomial time, a contraction model of Γt in GhAi. We have (r + 1)2 > r
trivially, so this contraction model contains a (2r + 7) × (2r + 7)-subgrid without vertices from NG (A).
A (2r + 7) × (2r + 7)-subgrid contains r + 3 nested connected separators S10 , . . . , Sr+3
                                                                                         0   between the
central branch set and the rest of the grid. Let v be any vertex from the central branch set of this
subgrid and Si be the union of vertices contracted into Si0 for i ∈ [r + 3]. By Observation 4.2, the
sets S1 , . . . , Sr+3 are nested connected (v, NG (A))-separators. Therefore each set Si , i ∈ [r + 3], is
v-planarizing.


                                                        32
     Suppose now that one is given a planar modulator S in G − v. By Lemma 6.4, one can find,
in time nO(r) , an r-locally optimal planar modulator S 0 in G − v of size at most |S|. Observe that
|S 0 ∩ A| ≤ r because otherwise (S 0 \ A) ∪ NG (A) would be a smaller planar modulator, contradicting
S 0 being r-locally optimal. Therefore there exists distinct indices i1 , i2 , i3 ∈ [r + 3] such that
Si1 , Si2 , Si3 are disjoint from S 0 . For each j ∈ [3] we have that RG−S 0 [v, Sij ] ⊆ A induces a planar
subgraph of G − S 0 . By the assumption G − S 0 − v is planar, so together, by Lemma 3.17, G − S 0
is planar. We have thus shown how to efficiently turn a planar modulator in G − v into a planar
modulator in G.

   Once we are able to reduce the treewidth of a planar subgraph with a small (constant) boundary,
we can use protrusion replacement to reduce its size. The setting in which we work implies some
convenient corollaries about the result of the replacement act, of which we make note in the following
lemma.

Lemma 6.6. Let γ be the function from Definition 6.1 for the Vertex planarization problem,
r be a constant and r0 = 27(2r + 7)(r + 1). There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given an
n-vertex graph G, a vertex set A ⊆ V (G), such that GhAi is planar and connected, NG (A) = r and
|A| ≥ γ(r0 ), outputs a graph G0 so the following hold.

   1. G0 is obtained from G by replacing NG [A] with some labeled r-boundaried graph H through
      some bijection λ : [r] → NG (A).

   2. H has less than γ(r0 ) vertices.

   3. mvp(G0 ) = mvp(G).

   4. For any vertex set X ⊆ V (G) \ NG [A], if G − X is planar and connected then G0 − X is planar
      and connected.

   5. Given G, G0 and a planar modulator S 0 in G0 one can, in time nO(r) , turn it into a planar
      modulator in G of size at most |S 0 |.

Proof. First, we reduce the treewidth of GhAi to be at most r0 using Lemma 6.5. While tw(GhAi) >
r0 , we can find a vertex v ∈ A such that any planar modulator S in G − v can be turned, in time
nO(r) , into a planar modulator in G of size at most |S|. Hence, removing v is safe and we can
continue the process on the graph G − v. Furthermore, there is at least one connected restricted
(v, NG (A))-separator in GhAi so removal of v does not disconnect any vertices from NG (A) within
GhAi. If some vertices get disconnected from NG (A) then they form a planar connected component
in G, which can be discarded. Therefore, we maintain connectivity of the graph GhAi. Let G0 be
obtained from G after the exhaustive application of Lemma 6.5 and A0 ⊆ A be obtained from A by
removing the irrelevant vertices. Then the set NG0 [A0 ] forms an r0 -protrusion. We can treat G0 as
obtained from G by replacing A. Note that mvp(G0 ) = mvp(G) because the lifting algorithm can
lift any solution losslessly. If |NG0 [A0 ]| < γ(r0 ), then we are done.
      Suppose that |NG0 [A0 ]| ≥ γ(r0 ). By Lemma 6.2, the Vertex planarization problem admits a
lossless protrusion replacer, so we can replace NG0 [A0 ] with a labeled r-boundaried graph H, with
at most γ(r0 ) vertices, through some labeling λ, obtaining a graph G0 . From the definition of a
lossless protrusion replacer we have that mvp(G0 ) ≤ mvp(G0 ) = mvp(G) and any planar modulator
S 0 in G0 can be turned, in polynomial time, into a planar modulator S0 in G0 of size at most
|S 0 | + mvp(G0 ) − mvp(G0 ). Thanks to Lemma 6.5, we can compute a planar modulator S in G of
size at most |S0 |, in time nO(r) .


                                                    33

     Finally we show that for any vertex set X ⊆ V (G) \ NG [A], if G − X is planar and connected

6.2    Outerplanar decomposition
In order to conveniently analyze plane graphs with large radial diameter we take advantage of the
concept of a decomposition into outerplanarity layers used by Jansen et al. [JPvL19] in their work
on kernelization for Multiway cut on planar graphs. We refer to the definitions from the full
version [JPvL18] of this article.

Definition 6.7 ([JPvL18, Definition 5.1]). Let G be a plane graph. The outerplanarity layers of G
form a partition of V (G) into L1 , . . . , Lm defined recursively. The vertices incident with the outer
face of G belong to layer L1 . If v ∈ V (G) lies on the outer face of the plane subgraph obtained
from G by removing L1 , . . . , Li , then v belongs to layer Li+1 . For a vertex v ∈ V (G), the unique
index i ∈ [m] for which v ∈ Li is the outerplanarity index of v and denoted idxG (v).

   A plane graph is k-outerplanar if this process partitions it into exactly k non-empty layers. A
graph is k-outerplanar if it admits a k-outerplanar embedding.

Definition 6.8 ([JPvL18, Definition 5.2]). For a plane graph G, let T be the simple graph obtained
by simultaneously contracting all edges uv whose endpoints belong to the same outerplanarity layer,
discarding loops and parallel edges. For a node u ∈ V (T ), let κ(u) ⊆ V (G) denote the vertex set
of G whose contraction resulted in u. Let idxG (u) denote the outerplanarity index that is shared by
all nodes in κ(u).

    We make note of several properties of this definition.



                                                      34
Lemma 6.9 ([JPvL18, Lemma 5.3]). For a connected plane graph G and its outerplanar decompo-
sition (T, κ) based on the outerplanarity layers L1 , . . . , Lm , the following holds:

   1. T is a tree.

   2. For any node u ∈ V (T ), the graph G[κ(u)] is connected.

   3. If x, y are distinct vertices of T , then for any internal node z of the unique (x, y)-path in T ,
      the set κ(z) is a restricted (κ(x), κ(y))-separator in G.

   4. There is a unique node x1 ∈ V (T ) such that κ(x1 ) = L1 .

   5. Root T at vertex x1 . If u ∈ V (T ) is a child of p ∈ V (T ), then idxG (u) = 1 + idxG (p).

    We call the pair (T, κ), where T is the tree rooted at x1 accordingly to Lemma 6.9, the outerplanar
decomposition of G. Note that it is uniquely defined for a given connected plane graph G. Let
δT denote the shortest path distance on the tree T . For distinct x, y ∈ V (T ) we define Tx,y P to be

the unique (x, y)-path in T and for non-adjacent x, y ∈ V (T ) we define Tx,y to be the connected
component of T − {x, y} containing the internal vertices of Tx,y  P . For a subtree T 0 of T we use the
              0                                           0
notation κ(T ) to denote the union of {κ(t) | t ∈ V (T )}. Note that NG (κ(Tx,y )) ⊆ κ(x) ∪ κ(y).

Observation 6.10 ([JPvL18, Observation 5.4]). Let G be a connected plane graph and (T, κ) be
its outerplanar decomposition. If u, v ∈ V (G) share a face, then either there is x ∈ V (T ) such that
u, v ∈ κ(x) or there are x, y ∈ V (T ) which are adjacent in T and u ∈ κ(x), v ∈ κ(y). Conversely, if
x is a child of y in T and v ∈ κ(x), then there exists u ∈ κ(y), so that u, v share a face.

    This observation allows us to make a link between the depth of the outerplanar decomposition
and the radial diameter of the graph. We define the depth of a rooted tree as the longest root-leaf
distance plus 1 (so a singleton tree has depth 1).

Lemma 6.11. Let G be a connected plane graph and (T, κ) be its outerplanar decomposition. If the
maximal depth in T is d, then the radial diameter of G is at most 2d − 1.

Proof. By Observation 6.10 any vertex in Li+1 shares a face with a vertex from Li . Let u, v ∈ V (G)
be any pair of vertices. We have idxG (u), idxG (v) ≤ d so they are at radial distance at most d − 1
from some vertices from L1 which are at radial distance 1, hence dG (u, v) ≤ 2d − 1.

   Since T is a contraction of G, when a set S ⊆ V (G) separates κ(x) from κ(y), it also separates
κ(x) from the image of the entire subtree behind y. We make note of this observation for the case
where we separate κ(x) from two directions at once.

Observation 6.12. Let G be a connected plane graph and (T, κ) be its outerplanar decomposition.
Let x, y, z ∈ V (T ) be distinct pairwise non-adjacent vertices such that z lies on the unique (x, y)-path
in T . Suppose that S ⊆ V (G) is a restricted (κ(z), κ(x) ∪ κ(y))-separator. Then the connected
component of G − S containing κ(z) is contained in κ(Tx,y ).

   We also state a strengthening of Lemma 6.9(3) which says that the separators within outerpla-
narity layers are in fact cycles.

Observation 6.13. Let G be a connected plane graph, (T, κ) be its outerplanar decomposition, and
x, y, z ∈ V (T ) be distinct. If z lies on the unique (x, y)-path in T , then there is a cycle C in G such
that V (C) ⊆ κ(z) and one of κ(x), κ(y) lies entirely in the interior of C and the other one in the
exterior of C.

                                                   35
6.3    Outerplanarity layers and vertex-disjoint paths
We will be working with an outerplanar decomposition (T, κ) of a plane graph G and families of
internally vertex-disjoint paths connecting two outerplanarity layers, so that one is drawn inside the
other one. First we show that paths in such a family can be assumed to cross each intermediate
outerplanarity layer only once.

Lemma 6.14. Let G be a connected plane graph, L1 be the set of vertices lying on the outer face of
G, and F ⊆ V (G) \ L1 induce a connected subgraph of G. Suppose that u, v ∈ L1 are non-adjacent
and connected by a path P with all its internal vertices disjoint from (L1 ∪ F ). Then there exists a
(u, v)-path P 0 such that V (P 0 ) ⊆ L1 and the internal vertices of P 0 belong to a connected component
of G − V (P ) which is disjoint from F .

Proof. As u, v are non-adjacent, the interior of P is non-empty. Since G[L1 ] is connected, there is
a (u, v)-path within L1 , which is internally disjoint from P . Therefore u, v belong to a common
biconnected component B of G and the subgraph B contains a cycle C1 such that V (C1 ) = L1 ∩V (B).
Removing non-adjacent u, v from C1 separates V (C1 ) into two non-empty sets S 1 , S 2 , which end up
in different connected components of G − V (P ) since u and v lie on the outer face. Since G[F ] is
connected, it is fully contained in one such component. We can thus use either S 1 or S 2 to make
the desired path P 0 .

    For a path P and a vertex set X, a segment of P in X is an inclusion-wise maximal subpath P 0
of P such that V (P 0 ) ⊆ X. We define S(P, X) to be the set of segments of P in X.

Lemma 6.15. Consider a connected plane graph G, its outerplanar decomposition (T, κ), and non-
adjacent x, y ∈ V (T ) such that x is an ancestor of y. Suppose we are given a family of internally
vertex-disjoint (κ(x), κ(y))-paths P. Then we can compute, in polynomial time, a family P 0 of
internally vertex-disjoint (κ(x), κ(y))-paths such that |P 0 | = |P| and for each P ∈ P 0 and z ∈ Tx,y
                                                                                                   P it

holds that |S(P, κ(z))| = 1.

Proof. By replacing each P ∈ P by the (κ(x), κ(y))-subpath between the last visit to κ(x) and
the first visit to κ(y), we can assume that the internal vertices of the paths in P belong to
P x,y ).PFor a family of internally vertex-disjoint κ(x), κ(y)-paths P we define the measure µ(P) =

    We apply this refinement exhaustively as long as there exist P ∈ P and z ∈ Tx,y         P with

|S(P, κ(z))| > 1. It is clear that the refinement step can be performed in polynomial time. At each
step the value of µ decreases so at some point the process terminates and gives the desired path
family.

    It is important that the sets considered in Lemma 6.15 are given by the consecutive outerplanarity
layers. For other choice of connected separators, such a claim might not be true.
    By inspecting the outerplanar decomposition (T, κ) of an r-boundaried plane graph H, we can
find the set of nodes in T whose images cover the boundary ∂(H), and then mark the LCA closure
of this set (recall Definition 3.8), thus marking at most 2r nodes. We consider the nodes which
were not marked and we want to compress the graph whenever there is a long path of non-marked
nodes. This translates to a situation where one group T1 of relevant vertices (that is, located in a
marked bag) lies on one outerplanarity layer, there is a deep well of nested outerplanarity layers,
and another group T2 of relevant vertices is located inside the well. We want to say that only the
O(k) layers closest to T1 and O(k) layers closest to T2 are important and the middle layers can be
compressed.
    Let S be some solution and suppose that there at least 4 internally vertex-disjoint (T1 , T2 )-paths
in H − S. We need an argument that in this case, for any vertex v in the middle layers that does
not lie on any of these four paths, we can draw two vertex-disjoint cycles around v that separate v
from all the relevant vertices, and in particular from ∂(H). We begin with a topological argument
that such cycles exist. We do not rely on Lemma 6.15 and give the argument in a general form.
Lemma 6.16. Let G be a plane graph, C1 , . . . , C6 be vertex-disjoint cycles in G, so that Ci+1 is
drawn inside Ci for i ∈ [5],Sand let PA , PB , PC , PD be vertex-disjoint (C1 , C6 )-paths. Suppose that a

proper exterior of C4 . Then there exist two vertex-disjoint cycles in G which are nested with respect
to v and separate v from V (C1 ) ∪ V (C6 ).

a path PX with X ∈ J. For ease of presentation, we abbreviate {A, B, C, D} by [D].

6.4    Compressing the outerplanarity layers
We focus on the case where x, y ∈ V (T ) are some marked vertices, x is an ancestor of y and Tx,y
contains no marked vertices (and thus its image contains no boundary vertices). If δT (x, y) is large,
we want to preserve just O(k) outerplanarity layers closest to κ(x), O(k) layers closest to κ(y),
and in the middle layers we want to preserve only the maximal family internally of vertex-disjoint
(κ(x), κ(y))-paths, discarding the remaining vertices and edges.
    We need to argue for the backward safeness of such a graph modification. Consider a solution S
such that |S ∩ κ(Tx,y )| ≤ k. If H − S contains at least 4 internally vertex-disjoint (κ(x), κ(y))-paths,


                                                     38
we can surround the modified areas with two cycles using Lemma 6.16 and argue that inserting
them back does not affect planarity. Otherwise we want to say that S ∩ κ(Tx,y ) is sufficiently large
so we can charge these vertices to “pay” for additional vertex removals which separate κ(x) from
κ(y) in H. The following statement remains correct if we assume the existence of just 4 internally
vertex-disjoint (κ(x), κ(y))-paths, instead of 6, but with a worse approximation factor of the lifting
algorithm. On the other hand, factor 2 can be improved to (1 + ε) if we assume that even more
paths exist but for the sake of keeping the presentation clear we do not optimize this constant.

Lemma 6.17. Let G be a graph, A ⊆ V (G), H be a connected boundaried plane graph given by
some plane embedding of GhAi, (T, κ) be the outerplanar decomposition of H, and k be an integer.
Consider vertices x, y ∈ V (T ) such that x is an ancestor of y, δT (x, y) > 4k + 11, κ(Tx,y ) ∩ ∂(H) = ∅,
and there are 6 internally vertex-disjoint (κ(x), κ(y))-paths in the graph Hhκ(Tx,y )i. There is a
polynomial-time algorithm that, given all the above, returns a vertex set VD ⊆ κ(Tx,y ) and an edge
set ED ⊆ EG (κ(Tx,y ), κ(Tx,y )) so that the following hold.

   1. For each vertex v ∈ κ(y) there exists a vertex u ∈ κ(x) so that the radial distance between u
      and v in H 0 = (H \ ED ) − VD is at most 4k + 11.

   2. The boundaried graph H 0 = (H \ ED ) − VD is connected.

   3. Given a planar modulator S 0 in G0 = (G \ ED ) − VD , such that |S 0 ∩ κ(Tx,y )| ≤ k, one can turn
      it, in polynomial time, into a planar modulator S in G such that |S| ≤ |S 0 | + 2 · |S 0 ∩ κ(Tx,y )|
      and S \ κ(Tx,y ) = S 0 \ κ(Tx,y ).

Proof. We set D = κ(Tx,y ), T1 = κ(x), T2 = κ(y), and J be the boundaried plane graph given by
the subgraph of H induced by D ∪ T1 ∪ T2 and the boundary T1 ∪ T2 . Note that NG (D) ⊆ T1 ∪ T2
and T1 , T2 are non-adjacent. Let d be the minimum size of a restricted (T1 , T2 )-separator in J. By
Menger’s theorem there exist d internally vertex-disjoint (T1 , T2 )-paths in J: P1 , . . . Pd . By the
assumption d ≥ 6. Furthermore, by Lemma 6.15 we can assume that each path Pi has only one
segment in each κ(z) for z ∈ Tx,y  P .

     Let q1 ∈ V (T ) be the vertex on path Tx,y P with distance δ (x, q ) exactly 2k + 5. Similarly,
                                                                       T     1
q2 ∈ V (T ) is the vertex on path Tx,y P with distance δ (y, q ) exactly 2k + 5. We define Q = T
                                                            T     2                                    q1 ,q2 .
By the assumption Q is non-empty. Let VQ = κ(Q); then VQ is a restricted (T1 , T2 )-separator in H
and also in J. We define ED to be the set of edges with at least one endpoint in VQ , which do not
belong to any path Pi for i ∈ [d]. The set VD is the set of vertices in VQ which become isolated in
H \ ED . See Figure 4 for an illustration. Let G0 , H 0 , J 0 denote the graphs obtained respectively from
G, H, J after removing the edges from ED and vertices from VD . We have that G0 = G∂ [A] ⊕ H 0 .
Note that H 0 is connected and J 0 still admits d internally vertex-disjoint (T1 , T2 )-paths.
     We will show that any planar modulator S 0 in G0 with bounded intersection with D can be lifted
to a planar modulator in G, by possibly increasing its size slightly. We consider two cases. First,
if removing vertices from S 0 ∩ D preserves at least 4 internally vertex-disjoint (T1 , T2 )-paths in J 0 ,
then we show that any region in which the graph modifications occurred can be surrounded by 2
cycles separating this region from the boundary of H. Then the planarity criterion can be applied
to argue that S 0 remains a planar modulator after undoing the graph modifications. Otherwise, the
set S 0 ∩ D is sufficiently large so we can charge it with adding to S 0 a restricted (T1 , T2 )-separator
in J. Then each leftover connected component in J is disconnected from either T1 or T2 , which
again allows us to apply the planarity criterion to justify undoing the graph modifications.
Claim 6.18. Let S 0 be a planar modulator in G0 such that |S 0 ∩ D| ≤ k and the minimum size of a
restricted (T1 , T2 )-separator in J 0 − (S 0 ∩ D) is at least 4. Then S 0 is also a planar modulator in G.

Claim 6.19. Let S 0 be a planar modulator in G0 such that |S 0 ∩ D| ≤ k and there exists a restricted
(T1 , T2 )-separator in J 0 − (S 0 ∩ D) of size at most 3. Let R be a minimum-size restricted (T1 , T2 )-
separator in J. Then S = S 0 ∪ R is also a planar modulator in G and |S| ≤ |S 0 | + 2 · |S 0 ∩ D|.

Proof. Recall that we have preserved the paths P1 , . . . , Pd during the graph modification and J 0 still


                                                        40
admits d ≥ 6 internally vertex-disjoint (T1 , T2 )-paths. The size of S 0 ∩ D is at least d − 3 ≥ 3 because
removing one vertex from D can decrease the flow between T1 and T2 (which are disjoint from D) by
at most one. We have |R| = d ≤ |S 0 ∩ D| + 3 ≤ 2 · |S 0 ∩ D| and so |S| ≤ |S 0 | + |R| ≤ |S 0 | + 2 · |S 0 ∩ D|,
as intended. Also, |S| ≤ |S 0 | + |R| ≤ |S 0 | + |S 0 ∩ D| + 3 ≤ 2k + 3. By the definition of Q, there exist
vertices q10 , q20 , q30 , q40 ∈ V (T ) lying in this order on the path Tx,y

     We will transform the graph J 0 − S back into J − S by undoing the vertex and edge removals so
that G0 − S is transformed into G − S, while maintaining planarity of the graph. First, inserting an
isolated vertex from VD does not affect planarity of a graph. Now consider a single insertion of an
edge e ∈ ED and let J 00 , G00 denote the graph obtained respectively from J 0 , G0 after the insertions
done so far, and assume inductively that S is a planar modulator in G00 \ e. We are going to prove
that S is a planar modulator in G00 . If both endpoints of e are disconnected from (T1 ∪ T2 ) \ S in

planar graph by assumption.
     Suppose otherwise. Let v ∈ VQ be an endpoint of e. Since R is a restricted (T1 , T2 )-separator
in J (and so in J 00 ) and R ⊆ S, v can be connected to at most one of T1 , T2 in J 00 − S. Suppose
w.l.o.g. that v is connected to some vertex from T1 in J 00 − S but none from T2 . By the definition
of VQ , every (T1 , v)-path in J must intersect κ(q10 ) and κ(q20 ). These sets are disjoint from S and
by Observation 6.13 there are two cycles separating v in G00 from any vertex from T1 \ S. By
Lemma 3.16 we conclude that G00 − S is planar. After undoing all the edge deletions we obtain that
G − S is planar. The claim follows.                                                                           J

     In the first case we do not change the size of the planar modulator and in the second case we
have |S| ≤ |S 0 | + 2 · |S 0 ∩ D|. Therefore the lifting algorithm can turn any solution to G0 into a
solution to G within the guarantees of the lemma.
     Finally, let us examine the radial distances in H 0 . By Observation 6.10 we have that whenever
x1 , x2 ∈ V (T ) are such that x1 is parent to x2 and v ∈ κ(x2 ) then there exists u ∈ κ(x1 ) so that u, v
share a face. Let us fix a vertex vy ∈ κ(y). By the observation above, there is a vertex v2 ∈ κ(q2 )
so that dH 0 (vy , v2 ) = dH (vy , v2 ) ≤ 2k + 5. As each path Pi , i ∈ [d], has only one segment in κ(q1 )
and one in κ(q2 ), it also has only one segment in VQ . The region between κ(q1 ) and κ(q2 ) in H 0
comprises d subdivided edges between κ(q1 ) and κ(q2 ). Hence, there is a vertex v1 ∈ κ(q1 ) which
shares a face with v2 in H 0 . By the same argument as before, there is a vertex vx ∈ κ(x) at radial
distance 2k + 5 from v1 in H 0 . This implies that dH 0 (vy , vx ) ≤ (2k + 5) + 1 + (2k + 5) = 4k + 11 and
concludes the proof.

    Our strategy is to mark the set of nodes in T whose images cover the boundary ∂(H) together
with its LCA closure. Suppose that x, y ∈ V (T ) were marked, x is an ancestor of y, and Tx,y contains
no marked vertices. If δT (x, y) is large and there are 6 internally vertex-disjoint (κ(x), κ(y))-paths,
we can use Lemma 6.17 to compress the middle layers. If the (κ(x), κ(y))-flow is less than 6, there
is a restricted (κ(x), κ(y))-separator of size at most 5. If the number of such separators is small,
we could just mark some more nodes in T . We cannot do this however if there is a long sequence
of small (κ(x), κ(y))-separators. But in this case the subgraph of H between the first and the last
separator is disjoint from ∂(H) and has boundary of size at most 10. Recall that the Vertex
planarization problem admits a lossless protrusion replacer (Lemma 6.2) which allows us to
compress planar subgraphs of small boundary (Lemma 6.6). We use this tool in a preprocessing
step to ensure that the distance between the first and last small separator cannot be too large.

Lemma 6.20. Let γ be the function from Definition 6.1 for the Vertex planarization problem.
There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph G, integers k, r, a set A ⊆ V (G), such

                                                      41
that GhAi is planar, G[A] is connected, and r = |NG (A)|, returns an r-boundaried plane graph H 0
so the following hold.
   1. The radial diameter of H 0 is at most 2 · (4k + γ(8019) + 12) · (6r + 5) and the graph H 0 \ ∂(H 0 )
      is connected.
   2. mvp(G0 ) ≤ mvp(G), where G0 is the graph obtained from G after replacing NG [A] with H 0 .
   3. Let A0 = V (H 0 ) \ ∂(H 0 ) denote the set of the new vertices inserted in place of A. There is
      a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a planar modulator S 0 in G0 such that |S 0 ∩ A0 | ≤ k,
      outputs a planar modulator S in G such that |S| ≤ |S 0 | + 2 · |S 0 ∩ A0 | and S \ A = S 0 \ A0 .
Proof. Let H denote a boundaried plane graph obtained by an arbitrary plane embedding of GhAi.
We shall perform a series of modifications of H which does not affect the boundary set. First, we
get rid of planar subgraphs with small boundary. We iterate over all sets X0 ⊆ V (H) \ ∂(H) of size
at most 10 and inspect whether there is a connected component C of H − X0 which (a) does not
contain any vertex from ∂(H), (b) has at least γ(8019) vertices. If we detect such a component C,
we invoke Lemma 6.6 for r = |NH (C)| ≤ 10 and replace NG [C] with a boundaried subgraph on less
than γ(27 · 27 · 11) = γ(8019) vertices. This does not affect the minimum vertex planarization number
and allows to perform lossless solution lifting in polynomial time. We perform such a replacement
exhaustively until we cannot find a pair (X0 , C) satisfying the requirements. By Lemma 6.6(4)
for X = V (G) \ A such a replacement does not affect planarity of the graph GhAi. By the same
argument for X = V (G) \ NG [A] we get that the replacement cannot disconnect the graph G[A]. In
each step, the size of the graph decreases, so at some point there are no more such pairs (X0 , C).
After this process terminates, we know that there is no separator of size at most 10, which would
disconnect a subgraph of H which does not touch the boundary and has at least γ(8019) vertices.
We keep the variable H to denote the graph after this modification.
      Let us now consider the outerplanar decomposition (T, χ) of H. For each v ∈ ∂(H) we mark
the node x ∈ V (T ) such that v ∈ κ(x). Let Y0 denote the set of marked nodes plus the root node,
|Y0 | ≤ r + 1, and Y1 = LCA(Y0 ) (recall Definition 3.8). By Lemma 3.9 we know that |Y1 | ≤ 2r + 2
and every connected component of T − Y1 has at most 2 neighbors. Furthermore, there can be
at most 2r + 1 such components with exactly 2 neighbors because contracting them forms a tree
on 2r + 2 vertices. Consider such a component TC ∈ CC(T − Y1 ), NT (TC ) = {x, y}. Then one of
the vertices x, y is an ancestor of the other one. Note that the path Tx,y               P is contained in N [T ].

     Now, we analyze the radial diameter of H0 . Let v ∈ ∂(H0 ) = ∂(H) and y ∈ Y2 be such that
v ∈ κ(y). Since the root node x1 belongs to Y2 and |Y2 | ≤ 6r +4, there a sequence y = y1 , . . . , ym = r
of at most m ≤ 6r + 4 vertices from Y2 so that yi+1 is an ancestor of yi and Tyi ,yi+1 ∩ Y2 = ∅. If

u0 ∈ κ(yi+1 ) such that their radial distance in H0 is at most 4k + 11. If Tyi ,yi+1 is a component of
type (a), then by Observation 6.10 we get the analogous property but with upper bound γ(8019) + 1.
Therefore v is at radial distance at most (4k + γ(8019) + 12) · (6r + 4) from some vertex on the
outer face.
     To analyze the situation for vertices from V (H0 ) \ ∂(H0 ) let us consider the outerplanar
decomposition (T0 , κ0 ) of H0 . By the estimation above, if v ∈ ∂(H0 ) = ∂(H) then idxH0 (v) ≤
(4k + γ(8019) + 12) · (6r + 4). Suppose there is a vertex u ∈ V (H0 ) \ ∂(H0 ) for which idxH0 (u) >
(4k + γ(8019) + 12) · (6r + 4) + k + 3. Then there are at least k + 3 outerplanarity layers around u
whose interiors are free of any boundary vertices. Consider the sequence u1 , u2 , . . . , uk+3 ∈ V (T0 ) of
the first ancestors of u in T0 . By Lemma 6.9(2) each set κ(ui ) induces a connected subgraph of G0 .
If v ∈ ∂(H0 ), then idxH0 (v) < idxH0 (qi ) and by Lemma 6.9(3) κ(ui ) is a restricted (u, v)-separator
in H0 . Hence, these sets form a nested sequence of (u, ∂(H0 ))-separators in H0 and thus these are
(u, NG0 (A))-separators in G0 . We infer that these sets are u-planarizing and by Lemma 3.17 the
vertex u is k-irrelevant. While such a vertex u exists we can safely remove it; let H 0 be the graph
obtained from H0 after these removals. The depth of the outerplanar decomposition tree of H 0 is
bounded by (4k + γ(8019) + 12) · (6r + 4) + k + 3 and the claim follows from Lemma 6.11.

    We will apply Lemma 6.20 to each connected component C ∈ CC(G − X) where X is a strong
planar modulator supplied by Proposition 5.7. We obtain some replacement HC for the boundaried
graph GhCi equipped with a plane embedding of moderate diameter. Because the lifting algorithm
from Lemma 6.20 increases the size of a solution only locally in C and does not affect the solution
outside C, we can perform the lifting iteratively, increasing the size of the solution only by a constant
factor in the end.

Proposition 6.21. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph G with an r-strong
planar modulator X of fragmentation s, and integer k, outputs a graph G0 together with a r-strong
planar modulator X 0 of fragmentation s and a family of plane embeddings of G0 hC 0 i for each
connected component C 0 ∈ CC(G0 − X 0 ), so that
    1. mvp(G0 ) ≤ mvp(G),
    2. for each connected component C 0 ∈ CC(G0 − X 0 ), the computed plane embedding has radial
       diameter O(kr),
    3. given G, G0 , and a planar modulator S 0 in G0 of size at most k, one can find, in polynomial
       time, a planar modulator S in G of size at most 3 · |S 0 |.
Proof. Let C1 , C2 , . . . , C` be some ordering of the connected components C ∈ CC(G − X). We
process each GhCi i with Lemma 6.20 iteratively. Namely, let G0 = G and Gi be obtained from Gi−1
by applying Lemma 6.20 to Gi−1 hCi i, for i ∈ [`]. We set G0 = G` . Let Ci0 be the set of new vertices
inserted in place of Ci and Hi0 denote the plane graph given by the computed embedding of Gi hCi0 i.
We have mvp(Gi ) ≤ mvp(Gi−1 ) so mvp(G` ) ≤ mvp(G). Furthermore, the radial diameter of Hi0
is O(kr) and we are guaranteed that the graphs Gi [Ci0 ] = Hi0 \ ∂(Hi0 ) are connected so we do not
increase the fragmentation of the modulator. As |NGi (Ci0 )| = |NGi−1 (Ci )| the modulator remains
r-strong.

of size at most k. We construct a series of sets S0 , . . . , S`−1Swhere Si is a modification of Si+1 so
that Si is a planar modulator in Gi of size at most |S` | + `j>i 2 · |S` ∩ Cj0 |. Moreover, we show
inductively that Si ∩ Cj0 = S` ∩ Cj0 for j ≤ i, which holds for i = `.
