7     Solution preservers
After the application of Proposition 6.21, we can assume that the considered graph G has a strong
planar modulator X ⊆ V (G) of fragmentation k O(1) and for each C ∈ CC(G − X) we are given a
plane embedding of GhCi with radial diameter k O(1) . In order to further simplify GhCi we take a
two-step approach. First, we mark a vertex set SC ⊆ C so that for any solution S we can replace the
set S ∩ C with a subset of SC ∪ NG (C) by paying a constant cost for each replacement. In particular,
we get that there is an O(1)-approximately optimal planar modulator S such that S ∩ C ⊆ SC .
Afterwards, in Section 8, we compress the subgraphs within C \ SC for which we know that the
approximately optimal solution does not remove any vertices inside them. We begin with formalizing
the concept of marking vertices to cover an approximate solution.
Definition 7.1. For a vertex set A ⊆ V (G), we say that SA ⊆ A is an α-preserver for A, if for
any planar modulator S in G there exists a planar modulator S 0 ⊆ (S \ A) ∪ NG (A) ∪ SA such that
S 0 \ NG [A] = S \ NG [A] and |S 0 | ≤ |S| + α · |S ∩ A|.
    We are going to construct these solution preservers recursively by reducing the task to more
structured boundaried graphs. We analyze this process by decomposing a boundaried graph H into
a set Y which can be regarded as an augmentation of the boundary ∂(H) and a family of smaller
boundaried graphs whose boundary lies on ∂(H) ∪ Y .

                                                     44
Definition 7.2. A boundaried decomposition of a boundaried graph H is a pair (Y, C), where
Y ⊆ V (H) \ ∂(H) and C is a partition of V (H) \ (∂(H) ∪ Y ), such that for each C ∈ C it holds that
NH (C) ⊆ ∂(H) ∪ Y .
    For example, when C = CC(H − (∂(H) ∪ Y )) is given by the connected components of H −
(∂(H) ∪ Y ) then (Y, C) is a boundaried decomposition of H. For our convenience, we will also
consider cases when some elements of C are disconnected. Usually, we begin with finding a set Y
that imposes some properties on boundaried graphs HhCi for C ∈ C. In order to bound the number
of subcases to recurse on, we would like to upper bound the maximal size of C in terms of Y . This
is not always possible, but at least we can bound the number of sets C ∈ C with more than two
neighbors.
Lemma 7.3 ([FLSZ19, Lem. 13.3]). Let G be a planar graph, X ⊆ V (G), and let N3 be a set of
vertices from V (G) \ X such that every vertex from N3 has at least three neighbors in X. Then,
|N3 | ≤ 2 · |X|.
   For a vertex set X ⊆ V (G) in a planar graph G, consider the graph obtained from G by
contracting each connected component of G − X into a single vertex. Since contractions preserve
planarity, we get the following corollary using the definition of fragmentation (Definition 3.3).
Lemma 7.4. For a planar graph G and a vertex set X ⊆ V (G), the fragmentation of X in G is at
most 2 · |X|.
    The following lemmas provide us with a framework to compute solution preservers recursively
using boundaried decompositions. The property S 0 \ NG [A] = S \ NG [A] in Definition 7.1 plays a
role in the second lemma because by modifying a solution locally we do not affect its intersection
with other parts of the decomposition.
Lemma 7.5. Consider a graph G and vertex sets A, Y ⊆ V (G). If SA ⊆ A is an α-preserver for
A, for some α ≥ 1, then SA ∪ Y is an α-preserver for A ∪ Y .
Proof. Let S ⊆ V (G) by any planar modulator in G. By the definition of an α-preserver, there
exists a planar modulator S 0 ⊆ V (G) such that S 0 ⊆ (S \ A) ∪ NG (A) ∪ SA , S 0 \ NG [A] = S \ NG [A],
and |S 0 | ≤ |S| + α · |S ∩ A| ≤ |S| + α · |S ∩ (A ∪ Y )|. The first property can be restated as
S 0 ⊆ (S \ (A ∪ Y )) ∪ NG (A ∪ Y ) ∪ (SA ∪ Y ) because inserting Y at the end covers all the modifications
to the first two terms. Since NG [A] ⊆ NG [A ∪ Y ] we get S 0 \ NG [A ∪ Y ] = S \ NG [A ∪ Y ]. This
satisfies all the three conditions of an α-preserver for A ∪ Y .

Lemma 7.6. Consider a graph G and vertex sets A, B ⊆ V (G), such that NG [A] ∩ B = ∅. If
SA ⊆ A, SB ⊆ B are α-preservers for respectively A, B, for some α ≥ 1, then SA ∪ SB is an
α-preserver for A ∪ B.
Proof. Let S ⊆ V (G) by any planar modulator in G. By the definition of an α-preserver, there
exists a planar modulator S1 ⊆ V (G) such that S1 ⊆ (S \ A) ∪ NG (A) ∪ SA , S1 \ NG [A] = S \ NG [A],
and |S1 | ≤ |S| + α · |S ∩ A|. The second property implies that S1 ∩ B = S ∩ B. Next, we apply
the definition for the set B and solution S1 . We obtain a planar modulator S2 ⊆ V (G) such
that S2 ⊆ (S1 \ B) ∪ NG (B) ∪ SB , S2 \ NG [B] = S1 \ NG [B], and |S2 | ≤ |S1 | + α · |S1 ∩ B| ≤
|S| + α · |S ∩ A| + α · |S ∩ B| = |S| + α · |S ∩ (A ∪ B)|.
    We check that S1 \ B ⊆ S \ (A ∪ B) ∪ NG (A) ∪ SA and NG (B) ∩ A = NG (A) ∩ B = ∅ so
S2 ⊆ S \ (A ∪ B) ∪ NG (A ∪ B) ∪ (SA ∪ SB ).
    Finally, both NG [A], NG [B] are subsets of NG [A ∪ B] so we trivially obtain S2 \ NG [A ∪ B] =
S1 \ NG [A ∪ B] = S \ NG [A ∪ B].

                                                   45
   From these two observations, we can easily obtain the following useful lemma.
Lemma 7.7. Consider a graph G, vertex set A ⊆ V (G), and a boundaried decomposition (Y, C) of
   S Suppose that for each set C ∈ C we are given an α-preserver SC ⊆ C for C. Then the set
GhAi.
Y ∪ C∈C SC is an α-preserver for A.
Proof. We fix any ordering C1 , . . . , Cm of C. For any distinct i, j ∈ [m] we have NG [Ci ] ∩ Cj = ∅.
From Lemma 7.6 S` we obtain that SC1 ∪ SC2 is   S`an α-preserver for C1 ∪ C2 and, by repeating this
argument, that i=1 SCi is an α-preserver for i=1 Ci , for each ` ∈ [m]. Next, we apply Lemma 7.5
to `i=1 Ci and Y , to get that C∈C SC ∪ Y is an α-preserver for their union, that is A.
   S                          S

    Suppose that G is a graph, X ⊆ V (G) is a planar modulator in G, and for each component C ∈
CC(G − X) we have computed an α-preserver SC ⊆ C. We can apply Lemma 7.7 to the boundaried
graph (G, ∅) (with the
                     S empty boundary) and the boundaried decomposition (X, CC(G − X)) to
get that X 0 = X ∪ C∈CC(G−X) SC is an α-preserver for V (G). In other words, for every planar
modulator S ⊆ V (G) in G, including the optimal one, there exists a planar modulator S 0 in G such
that S 0 ⊆ X 0 and |S 0 | ≤ α · |S|. This will allow us to restrict our attention only to the approximate
solutions contained in X 0 .

7.1    Reduction to single-faced boundaried graphs
In this section, we show how to reduce the task of constructing a solution preserver for GhAi with a
given embedding of bounded diameter to the case where in the embedding of GhAi all the boundary
vertices lie on the outer face. As an intermediate step, we shall consider circumscribed boundaried
plane graphs, which are slightly more general than single-faced plane graphs. This reduction will
also come in useful in Section 8.
    As we work with boundaried plane graphs of bounded radial diameter, we can mark a bounded-
size set of faces covering the boundary and forming a connected area on the plane. Then the
remaining parts of the graph interact with their boundaries only through the outer faces. Recall the
definition of a radial graph from Section 3.
Definition 7.8. Let G be a plane graph and let Rad(G) = (V (G) ∪ F, E) be the radial graph of G.
We say that S ⊆ V (G) is radially connected if there exists F 0 ⊆ F such that Rad(G)[S ∪ F 0 ] is
connected.
    Given a set of vertices in a plane graph of moderate radial diameter we want to find its superset
that is radially connected. A bound on the radial diameter translates to a bound on the diameter of
the radial graph. We will take advantage of the following simple observation about bounded-diameter
graphs.
Lemma 7.9. Let G be a connected graph of diameter bounded by d. Then for any non-empty vertex
set S ⊆ V (G) there exists S 0 ⊇ S such that G[S 0 ] is connected and |S 0 | ≤ d · |S|. Furthermore, such
a set S 0 can be found in polynomial time when G and S are given.
Proof. We prove by induction on ` that if G[S] has ` connected components, there is a connected
superset S 0 ⊇ S of size at most |S| + `(d − 1). This proves that any set S has a connected superset
of size at most d · |S| since G[S] has at most ` ≤ |S| connected components, and therefore gives the
lemma.
    If ` = 1 then S 0 = S suffices. Otherwise, let C1 , C2 be distinct connected components of G[S] and
let v1 ∈ C1 , v2 ∈ C2 . Since the diameter of G is at most d, there is a (v1 , v2 )-path P in G of length at
most d, which has at most d−1 internal vertices I. Then S∪I has at most `−1 connected components.

                                                    46
By induction, there is a connected superset S 0 of S ∪I on at most |S ∪I|+(`−1)·(d−1) ≤ |S|+`(d−1)
vertices, which concludes the proof. The inductive argument easily turns into a recursive polynomial-
time algorithm.
Lemma 7.10. Let G be a plane graph of radial diameter bounded by d. Then for any vertex set
S ⊆ V (G) there exists S 0 ⊇ S such that S 0 is radially connected in G and |S 0 | ≤ |S| · (2d + 2).
Furthermore, the set S 0 can be found in polynomial time when G and S are given.
Proof. Let Rad(G) = (V (G) ∪ F, E) be the radial graph of G. Note that Rad(G) is always connected,
even if G is not. Consider some pair u, v ∈ V (G). By assumption dG (u, v) ≤ d and so there
exists a (u, v)-path in Rad(G) of length at most 2d, as any jump between vertices that share a
face f can be emulated in Rad(G) by visiting the vertex representing f . Furthermore, each vertex
from F is adjacent in Rad(G) to some vertex from V (G), so Rad(G) has diameter at most 2d + 2.
By Lemma 7.9, there exists a set SR ⊇ S, SR ⊆ V (Rad(G)), so that Rad(G)[SR ] is connected
and |SR | ≤ |S| · (2d + 2), and this set can be efficiently computed. The claim follows by taking
S 0 = SR ∩ V (G).
    We proceed with decomposing a boundaried plane graph of moderate diameter into circumscribed
boundaried plane graphs by augmenting the boundary to be radially connected. Recall Definition 3.11
of a circumscribed boundaried plane graph H: all vertices of ∂(H) must be embedded in the outer
face of H − ∂(H).
Lemma 7.11. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a boundaried plane graph H of
radial diameter at most d and a (possibly empty) vertex set Y ⊆ V (H) \ ∂(H), outputs a vertex set
Y 0 ⊆ V (H) \ ∂(H) of size at most (2d + 2) · (|Y | + |∂(H)|), such that for each connected component
C of H − (∂(H) ∪ Y 0 ) the boundaried plane graph HhCi is circumscribed.
Proof. We apply Lemma 7.10 for S = ∂(H) ∪ Y , to find a set S 0 ⊇ ∂(H) of size at most (2d + 2) ·
(|Y | + |∂(H)|), which is radially connected in H. Consider a connected component C of H − S 0 and
the plane embedding of H truncated to the subgraph induced by C. Suppose there are at least two
faces of H[C] containing vertices from NH (C) (which is a subset of S 0 ). Then there exists a cycle in
H − S 0 with at least one vertex of S 0 in the interior and at least one vertex of S 0 in the exterior.
This contradicts the fact that S 0 is radially connected in H. We infer that there is only one face of
H[C] which contains vertices from NH (C), hence the graph HhCi is circumscribed. Note that we
can always modify the embedding by swapping the outer face with any other chosen face. The
claim follows by taking Y 0 = S 0 \ ∂(H).
    We are already close to the case with the boundary lying on the outer face. There is however
a subtle difference between circumscribed and single-faced boundaried plane graphs. If H is
circumscribed, then the edges incident to the boundary of H may separate some vertices of ∂(H)
from the outer face of H.
    We shall employ Lemma 3.7 to decompose H into boundaried subgraphs adjacent to at most two
vertices from ∂(H). We want to say that such a boundaried subgraph HhCi becomes single-faced
after discarding these two special vertices. This is done by exploiting minimality of a particular
separator which guarantees that there exists a subgraph disjoint from NH [C], that can be made
connected without violating planarity, adjacent to each vertex in NH (C). Contracting this subgraph
gives the closure graph of HhCi and allows us to use the criterion from Observation 3.13.
    In order to use Lemma 3.7 we need a tree decomposition of width k O(1) . Such a decomposition
is computed in Section 4 but later we apply several graph modifications in Section 6, including
protrusion replacement. This might invalidate the tree decomposition and we need to compute it
anew.

                                                  47
Lemma 7.12. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given an r-boundaried circumscribed
plane graph H of radial diameter at most d, such that r ≥ 3, outputs a vertex set Y ⊆ V (H) \ ∂(H)
of size at most 18 · dr, such that for each connected component C of H − (∂(H) ∪ Y ) it holds that
   1. |NH (C) ∩ ∂(H)| ≤ 2, and
   2. the boundaried graph HhCi − (NH (C) ∩ ∂(H)) is single-faced.
Proof. The graph H has at most d outerplanarity layers, so its treewidth is at most 3d − 1 [Bod98].
We invoke the polynomial-time 32 -approximation algorithm for treewidth on planar graphs [ST94]3
to compute a tree decomposition (T, χ) of H of width at most η = 23 (3d − 1).
    We supply the computed tree decomposition to Lemma 3.7 for S = ∂(H) and obtain a vertex
set Y ⊆ V (H) \ ∂(H) such that |Y | ≤ 4(η + 1) · r ≤ 18 · dr and each connected component of
H − (∂(H) ∪ Y ) has at most 2 neighbors in ∂(H) and at most 2η neighbors in Y . The latter property
is not used in this proof.
    In order to ensure condition (2) of the lemma, we need to trim the set Y . Consider the following
reduction rule: while there exists a vertex v ∈ Y such that Y \ {v} still satisfies condition (1),
remove v from Y . Let Y 0 ⊆ Y be the set obtained by exhaustive application of the reduction rule.
Observe that Y 0 6= ∅ because otherwise the only connected component of H − ∂(H) would have
r ≥ 3 neighbors in ∂(H) which does not satisfy condition (1).
    Consider a connected component C of H − (∂(H) ∪ Y 0 ) and v ∈ NH (C) ∩ Y 0 . Since v has not
been removed in the application of the reduction rule, there is a different connected component of
H − (∂(H) ∪ Y 0 ) which is adjacent to v and to some t ∈ ∂(H). Let H 1 be obtained from H by (1)
removing NH (C)∩∂(H), and (2) replacing ∂(H)\NH (C) with a single vertex u adjacent to entire set
NH (∂(H)). This operation can be seen as inserting a vertex adjacent to a subset of vertices lying on
the outer face of H − ∂(H), so H 1 is planar. By the observation above, the set RH 1 (u, Y 0 ) is adjacent
to each v ∈ NH 1 (C) ∩ Y 0 and it is disjoint from C. Let H 2 be obtained from H 1 by contracting
the connected vertex set RH 1 (u, Y 0 ) into u. This graph contains HhCi − (NH (C) ∩ ∂(H)) as a
boundaried subgraph H 2 hCi, together with u 6∈ C adjacent to each vertex in NH 2 (C). Therefore,
H 2 is the closure graph of HhCi − (NH (C) ∩ ∂(H)) which, as H 2 is planar and connected, implies
that HhCi − (NH (C) ∩ ∂(H)) is single-faced (see Observation 3.13).

Retrieving solution preservers from boundaried decompositions We have shown how to
decompose the bounded-diameter case into the circumscribed case, and the circumscribed case into
the single-faced case. Now we want to use Lemma 7.7 to “backpropagate” these reductions and
retrieve the solution preservers. We first state a crucial proposition which allows us to construct a
solution preserver for a single-faced boundaried graph.
Proposition 7.13. Let A ⊆ V (G) be such that GhAi is a single-faced boundaried graph. Then
there exists a set SA ⊆ A of size at most |NG (A)|2 which is a 168-preserver for A. Furthermore, SA
can be computed in polynomial time.
    In order to keep the focus of the current section, we postpone the proof of Proposition 7.13 to the
end of Section 7.3 and first discuss its implications. As the case of a circumscribed boundaried graph
can be reduced to the case with a single-faced boundaried graph via Lemma 7.12, our framework
yields a construction of solution preservers for circumscribed boundaried graphs.
    This construction may require removal of at most two vertices from the obtained boundaried
subgraph GhAi to make it single-faced. This however does not impose much trouble: if S is a
  3
    The work [ST94] focuses on computing branchwidth exactly on planar graphs and the 32 -approximation for
treewidth follows from the close relation between treewidth and branchwidth.


                                                    48
solution and S ∩ A is non-empty we can “charge” this part of the solution to pay for the removal of
the two vertices, turning an α-preserver into an (α + 2)-preserver. Similarly we argue that when
|NG (A)| ≤ 2 then if S ∩ A is non-empty then we may just remove NG (A) so in this case the empty
set is a 2-preserver for A.
Lemma 7.14. Consider a graph G, a vertex set A ⊆ V (G) such that |NG (A)| = r ≥ 3, and a
circumscribed embedding of GhAi of radial diameter at most d. Then there exists a 170-preserver
SA ⊆ A for A of size O(d3 r3 ). Furthermore, SA can be computed in polynomial time when given
G, A, and the embedding of GhAi.
Proof. We apply Lemma 7.12 to compute a set Y ⊆ A of size at most 18 · dr, such that for each
connected component C of GhAi − (NG (A) ∪ Y ) there exists a set DC = NG (C) ∩ NG (A) of size at
most 2 so that the boundaried graph GhCi − DC is single-faced. Let C be the family of connected
components of GhAi − (NG (A) ∪ Y ). Then (Y, C) is a boundaried decomposition of GhAi. We
partition C into families C≤2 , comprising those components that have at most 2 neighbors, and C≥3 ,
which contains all the remaining components.
     For C ∈ C≤2 a 2-preserver is given by the empty set: for any planar modulator S ⊆ V (G)
satisfying |S ∩ C| ≥ 1 we can replace S ∩ C with NG (C). Consider now a component C ∈ C≥3 . Let
G0 = G − DC ; we have that G0 hCi = GhCi − DC is a single-faced boundaried graph. We apply
Proposition 7.13 to compute a 168-preserver SC ⊆ C for C in G0 of size at most |NH (C)|2 = O(d2 r2 ).
We claim that SC is a 170 preserver for C in G. To see this, consider a planar modulator S ⊆ V (G)
in G satisfying |S ∩ C| ≥ 1. Then S1 = S \ DC is a planar modulator in G0 . By the definition of an
α-preserver, there exists a planar modulator S2 in G0 , such that (1) S2 ⊆ (S1 \ C) ∪ NG0 (C) ∪ SC ,
(2) S2 \ NG0 [C] = S1 \ NG0 [C], and (3) |S2 | ≤ |S1 | + 168 · |S1 ∩ C|. We set S3 = S2 ∪ DC , so
|S3 | ≤ |S| + 170 · |S ∩ C|. It is a planar modulator in G and, since DC ⊆ NG (C), it is a correct
replacement for S.                                S
     By Lemma 7.7 we obtain that SA = Y ∪ C∈C≥3 SC is a 170-preserver for A. We estimate
|C≥3 | ≤ 2 · |NG (A) ∪ Y | = O(dr) with Lemma 7.4 so the total size of SA is O(d3 r3 ).

    Finally, we employ the reduction from Lemma 7.11 to extend the construction above to the case
of boundaried plane graphs with bounded diameter. The following proposition encapsulates all the
results about solution preservers into a statement to be used in the main proof.
Proposition 7.15. Consider a graph G, a vertex set A ⊆ V (G) such that |NG (A)| = r, and a
plane embedding of GhAi of radial diameter at most d. Then there exists a 170-preserver SA ⊆ A
for A of size O(d7 r4 ). Furthermore, SA can be computed in polynomial time when G, A, and the
embedding of GhAi are given.
Proof. Let H be a boundaried plane graph of radial diameter at most d given by the embedding
of GhAi. We apply Lemma 7.11 to H with Y = ∅ to compute a set Y 0 of size at most (2d + 2) · r
such that for each connected component C of H − (∂(H) ∪ Y 0 ) the boundaried plane graph HhCi
is circumscribed. Let C be the family of connected components of H − (∂(H) ∪ Y ). We partition
C into families C≤2 , comprising those components that have at most 2 neighbors, and C≥3 , which
contains all the remaining components. We proceed similarly as in Lemma 7.14.
    For C ∈ C≤2 a 2-preserver is given by the empty set: for any planar modulator S ⊆ V (G)
satisfying |S ∩ C| ≥ 1 we can replace S ∩ C with NG (C). For each component C ∈ C≥3 we have
that |NH (C)| ≤ |Y ∪ ∂(H)| = O(dr). Furthermore, the radial diameter bound from GhAi transfers
to GhCi. We take advantage of Lemma 7.14 toScompute a 170-preserver SC ⊆ C of size O(d6 r3 ).
    By Lemma 7.7 we obtain that SA = Y ∪ C∈C≥3 SC is a 170-preserver for A. We estimate
|C≥3 | ≤ 2 · |NG (A) ∪ Y | = O(dr) with Lemma 7.4 so the total size of SA is O(d7 r4 ).

                                                 49
7.2    Inseparability in single-faced boundaried graphs
This section, together with the following one, are devoted to proving Proposition 7.13. To this end, we
first develop a framework of inseparability in single-faced boundaried graphs and reduce computing
a solution preserver to the case where the given single-faced boundaried graph is inseparable. In the
next section, we will focus on the inseparable case.
     Given a single-faced boundaried plane graph H we can consider a natural cyclic ordering of
∂(H). Since the vertices of ∂(H) lie on the outer face, we can augment the embedding by inserting a
special vertex uH adjacent to all vertices in ∂(H). This operation turns H into its closure graph H      b
(see Observation 3.13). After this operation we get a unique cyclic ordering of the neighbors of the
special vertex uH , which are the vertices from ∂(H). When considering a single-faced boundaried
plane graph H, we always implicitly assume a fixed cylic ordering of ∂(H), given by some embedding
of H.b For such an ordering, we define a canonical division as a partition ∂(H) = T1 ] T2 such
that T1 , T2 are non-empty connected segments in the cyclic ordering of ∂(H). We say that sets
T1 , T2 ⊆ T are non-crossing if they are non-empty and there exists a canonical division (T10 , T20 ) such
that T10 ⊇ T1 , T20 ⊇ T2 .

Observation 7.16. Consider a single-faced boundaried plane graph H and a canonical division
(T1 , T2 ) of ∂(H). Let H 00 be obtained by adding two new vertices t1 , t2 , adjacent to T1 and T2 ,
respectively. Then H 00 is planar.

    The following definition plays a key role in this section.

Definition 7.17. A c-separation (T1 , T2 , S) in a boundaried graph H is given by an unrestricted
(T1 , T2 )-separator S, such that T1 , T2 ⊆ ∂(H) are non-empty and disjoint, and c·|S| ≤ min(|T1 |, |T2 |).
We say that a boundaried graph H is c-inseparable if it does not admit a c-separation.
     Given a single-faced boundaried plane graph H, we say that a c-separation (T1 , T2 , S) is canonical
if (T1 , T2 ) is a canonical division of ∂(H) and S is an inclusion-wise minimal unrestricted (T1 , T2 )-
separator.

    Our current goal is to reduce the task of finding solution preservers into the case when the
given single-faced boundaried graph is c-inseparable for some constant c. As long as some c-
separation exists, ideally we would like to find it and recurse on the both sides of the separator.
It is however unclear whether a c-separation can be detected in polynomial time or whether the
obtained boundaried subgraphs are always single-faced. First we show that when we can separate
some non-crossing subsets of the boundary with a small separator, we can do the same for some
canonical division.

Lemma 7.18. Let H be a single-faced boundaried plane graph and T1 , T2 ⊆ ∂(H) be non-crossing.
Suppose there exists an unrestricted (T1 , T2 )-separator S, such that c · |S| ≤ min(|T1 |, |T2 |). Then H
admits a canonical c-separation.

Proof. Let T10 ⊇ T1 , T20 ⊇ T2 be disjoint segments of ∂(H) witnessing the non-crossing property.
Let H 00 be obtained from H by adding a new vertex t1 adjacent to all v ∈ T1 and a new vertex t2
adjacent to all v ∈ T2 . Since T1 , T2 are non-crossing, H 00 is planar by Observation 7.16. We can
assume that each vertex v ∈ T10 either belongs to T1 or to R = RH 00 (t2 , S) \ {t2 }, since if neither
holds then we can add v to T1 while preserving the preconditions to the lemma.
   We introduce some terminology for the rest of the proof. Suppose V1 , V2 ⊆ T1 are two disjoint
segments of terminals. Then the set ∂(H) \ (V1 ∪ V2 ) comprises of two segments U1 , U2 (U1 possibly
empty) such that T2 ⊆ U2 . We say that a vertex v ∈ ∂(H) lies between V1 and V2 when v ∈ U1 .


                                                    50
    We divide T1 into a sequence of (maximal) segments Q1 , . . . , Qm , ordered with respect to
the embedding, such that the boundary vertices between Qi and Qi+1 belong to R. Let Ci be
the set of vertices reachable from any v ∈ Qi in H 00 without going through R or t1 , that is,
Ci = RH 00 (Qi , R ∪ {t1 }).
    We claim that the sets (Ci )m     i=1 are pairwise disjoint. Assume otherwise that Ci ∩ Cj 6= ∅ for
some i < j. Then there exist vertices v1 ∈ Qi and v3 ∈ Qj connected by a path P1,3 in V (H) − R.
Let v2 ∈ T10 ∩ R be some vertex lying between Qi and Qj . Since v2 ∈ R, there exists a path
P2,4 connecting v2 to some vertex v4 ∈ T20 such that V (P ) ⊆ R. Note that the numbering of
vertices v1 , v2 , v3 , v4 obeys the cyclic ordering of terminals given by the single-faced embedding.
Let H0 be a graph obtained from H by inserting edges v1 v2 , v2 v3 , v3 v4 , v4 v1 and a new vertex v5
adjacent {v1 , v2 , v3 , v4 }. By the choice of the ordering, H0 is planar. The path P1,3 within V (H) \ R
connecting v1 and v3 is disjoint from the path P2,4 connecting v2 and v4 within R. By contracting
the paths P1,3 , P2,4 to edges we obtain a minor model of K5 in H0 , which is a contradiction with
the assumption that Ci ∩ Cj 6= ∅.
    Using the disjointness property we finish the proof. Let Si = S ∩ Ci for i ∈ [m]. The sets (Si )m      i=1
are pairwise disjoint and the sets (Qi )m  i=1  sum  up to T1 , hence the assumption c · |S| ≤ min(|T1 |, |T2 |)
implies that c · |Si | ≤ |Qi | for some i ∈ [m]. The set Si is an unrestricted (Qi , R)-separator in H
and an unrestricted (Qi , Qj )-separator in H for each j =       6 i because Ci ∩ Cj = ∅. Therefore, Si is
an unrestricted (Qi , ∂(H) \ Qi ) separator. Since c · |Si | ≤ c · |S| ≤ |T2 | ≤ |∂(H) \ Qi |, we obtain a
canonical c-separation.

   We now show that the existence of a (2c)-separation implies the existence of a c-separation of
non-crossing subsets, which in turn implies the existence of a canonical c-separation.

Lemma 7.19. Let H be a single-faced boundaried graph, T1 , T2 ⊆ ∂(H) be disjoint and non-empty,
and S be an unrestricted (T1 , T2 )-separator, such that 2c · |S| ≤ min(|T1 |, |T2 |). Then H admits a
canonical c-separation with respect to any given single-faced embedding.

Proof. Let us fix a single-faced embedding of H. Due to Lemma 7.18, it suffices to show that S
is an unrestricted (Q1 , Q2 )-separator for some non-crossing Q1 , Q2 ⊆ ∂(H), such that c · |S| ≤
min(|Q1 |, |Q2 |).
    Since |T2 | ≥ 2c·|S|, we can find two segments of ∂(H) that each have at least c·|S| vertices from T2 .
Hence there exists a canonical division (Q01 , Q02 ) of ∂(H), such that c · |S| ≤ min(|Q01 ∩ T2 |, |Q02 ∩ T2 |).
As the segments (Q01 , Q02 ) partition T1 ⊆ ∂(H) and |T1 | ≥ 2c · |S|, we have c · |S| ≤ |Q01 ∩ T1 |
or c · |S| ≤ |Q02 ∩ T1 |. Suppose this inequality holds for Q0i , i ∈ {1, 2}. Let Q1 = Q0i ∩ T1 and
Q2 = T2 \ Q0i . Since Q1 ⊆ T1 and Q2 ⊆ T2 , the set S is an unrestricted (Q1 , Q2 )-separator.

    As the number of canonical divisions is quadratic in the size of the boundary, we can find a
canonical c-separation in polynomial time. Together with Lemma 7.19, this yields a 2-approximation
algorithm for finding separations.

Lemma 7.20. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a single-faced boundaried graph
H and an integer c > 0, either correctly concludes that H is (2c)-inseparable or finds a canonical
c-separation with respect to some single-faced embedding of H.

Proof. Let us start with constructing a single-faced embedding of H: it can be done in polynomial
time since it suffices to construct any plane embedding of the closure of H (see Observation 3.13).
By Lemma 7.19, a single-faced boundaried graph either is (2c)-inseparable or admits a canonical
c-separation with respect to any single-faced embedding. We enumerate all |∂(H)|2 -many canonical
divisions with respect to the constructed embedding. For each canonical division (T1 , T2 ) we check

                                                      51
if there exists S such that (T1 , T2 , S) is a canonical c-separation. This task reduces to adding two
new vertices t1 , t2 , adjacent respectively to T1 and T2 , and finding a minimum (t1 , t2 )-separator. If
for some canonical division (T1 , T2 ) we find S such that c · |S| ≤ min(|T1 |, |T2 |), we can return it: it
must be an inclusion-wise minimal unrestricted (T1 , T2 )-separator. Otherwise we report that H is
(2c)-inseparable.

     Whenever we find a canonical c-separation we want to augment the boundary by marking
the vertices in the separator to split the given single-faced boundaried graphs into two smaller
single-faced boundaried graphs. We will add the marked vertices to the solution preserver and we
proceed recursively. A priori this construction may mark a large number of vertices so we need
some kind of a measure to ensure that the constructed solution preserver cannot be too large. Let
t be the size of the original boundary, s be the size of the separator, and t1 , t2 be the boundary
sizes of the two smaller single-faced boundaried graphs on which we recurse. Because we work with
a canonical c-separation we have that c · s ≤ min(t1 , t2 ). This implies that even when t1 + t2 > t
these numbers are in some sense balanced. It turns out that it suffices to take c = 4 to ensure that
t21 + t22 < t2 which provides us with a measure that decreases in each step and governs the number
of possible recursive splits.

Lemma 7.21. Let s ≥ 0, t > 0, t1 > 0, t2 > 0 be integers satisfying t1 ≥ 4s, t2 ≥ 4s, t + 2s ≥ t1 + t2 .
Then t2 ≥ t21 + t22 + s + 1.

Proof. First consider the case s = 0. We have t ≥ t1 + t2 > 0 so t2 ≥ (t1 + t2 )2 = t21 + t22 + 2t1 t2 ≥
t21 + t22 + 1 because t1 , t2 are positive.
     Now consider the case s ≥ 1. As t1 + t2 − 2s ≥ 6s ≥ 0, the inequality t ≥ t1 + t2 − 2s is
maintained with both sides squared. We enroll the squared expression, rearrange the terms, and
bound 4s2 ≥ s + 1.

                  t2 ≥ (t1 + t2 − 2s)2 = t21 + t22 + 4s2 + 2t1 t2 − 4st1 − 4st2
                      = t21 + t22 + 4s2 + t1 (t2 − 4s) + t2 (t1 − 4s) ≥ t21 + t22 + s + 1.

This concludes the proof.

    Now we show that when splitting a single-faced boundaried graph along a canonical separation,
we indeed obtain two smaller single-faced boundaried graphs. We do not construct their embeddings
directly but rather refer to the convenient criterion from Observation 3.13. Note that the definition
of a single-faced boundaried graph does not require it to be connected although connectivity will
later follow from being c-inseparable.

Lemma 7.22. Let H be a single-faced boundaried plane graph and let (T1 , T2 , S) be a canonical
4-separation. Then there exist sets V1 , V2 ⊆ V (H), so that

   1. (V1 , V2 ) is a partition of V (H) \ (∂(H) ∪ S),

   2. NH (V1 ) ⊆ T1 ∪ S and NH (V2 ) ⊆ T2 ∪ S,

   3. HhV1 i, HhV2 i are single-faced boundaried graphs,

   4. |NH (V1 )|2 + |NH (V2 )|2 + |S| + 1 ≤ |∂(H)|2 .

Furthermore, the sets V1 , V2 can be found in polynomial time, when H and (T1 , T2 , S) are given.



                                                    52
Proof. If |S| = 0, then T1 , T2 are disconnected in H. We can group the vertices of H − ∂(H) into
two sets V1 , V2 , where NH (Vi ) ⊆ Ti for i ∈ {1, 2}. Since T1 , T2 are non-empty by Definition 7.17, the
numbers 0, |∂(H)|, |T1 |, |T2 |, satisfy the prerequisites of Lemma 7.21, and so |NH (V1 )|2 + |NH (V2 )|2 +
|S| + 1 ≤ |∂(H)|2 . Since H is single-faced, the same is easily seen to hold for HhV1 i and HhV2 i.
      Let us assume |S| ≥ 1 for the rest of the proof. Consider the connected components of
H − (∂(H) ∪ S). None of them can have neighbors in both T1 and T2 . Therefore we can group the
vertices of V (H) \ (∂(H) ∪ S) into two sets V1 , V2 , where Vi is the union of the components with
neighbors in Ti ∪ S. If a component has neighbors only in S, we can choose its allocation arbitrarily.
      We are going to show that HhV1 i is single-faced—the proof for HhV2 i is symmetric. Note
that NH (V1 ) ⊆ T1 ∪ S. Let H0 be obtained from H by adding two new vertices t1 , t2 adjacent to
respectively T1 and T2 . Then H0 is planar and by Definition 7.17 S is an inclusion-wise minimal
(t1 , t2 )-separator in H0 . Therefore, each vertex from S is adjacent to both RH0 (t1 , S) and RH0 (t2 , S).
Let H1 be obtained from H0 by

   1. adding an edge t1 t2 —this preserves planarity,

   2. contracting RH0 (t2 , S) into t2 ,

   3. contracting the edge t1 t2 .

The graph H1 is planar and contains HhV1 i as a boundaried subgraph H1 hV1 i, plus one additional
vertex adjacent to every vertex in T1 ∪ S ⊇ NH1 (V1 ). Also, H1 is a contraction of the closure H        b of H,
hence it is planar and connected. Finally, let us remove from H1 all the vertices of (T1 ∪ S) \ NH1 (V1 ),
which trivially preserves planarity and preserves connectivity through t1 . The resulting graph is
isomorphic to the closure of HhV1 i. We have thus obtained that the closure of HhV1 i is planar and
connected so by Observation 3.13 the boundaried graph H is single-faced.
     Finally, let us prove the inequality. Since (T1 , T2 , S) is a 4-separation, it holds that min(|T1 |, |T2 |) ≥
4 · |S|. As |T1 ∪ S| + |T2 ∪ S| ≤ |∂(H)| + 2 · |S|, the numbers |S|, |∂(H)|, |T1 ∪ S|, |T2 ∪ S| satisfy the
prerequisites of Lemma 7.21, and so |T1 ∪ S|2 + |T2 ∪ S|2 + |S| + 1 ≤ |∂(H)|2 . Since NH (Vi ) ⊆ Ti ∪ S
for i ∈ {1, 2}, the claim follows.

    We are ready to decompose a single-faced boundaried graph H into boundaried subgraphs which
are single-faced and 8-inseparable. We define a single-faced decomposition of H as a boundaried
decomposition (Y, C) of H (recall Definition 7.2), such that for each C ∈ C it holds that HhCi is
single-faced.

Lemma 7.23. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a single-faced boundaried graph H,
returns its single-faced decomposition (Y, C), so that |Y | ≤ |∂(H)|2 and for each C ∈ C it holds that
HhCi is 8-inseparable.

Proof. We decompose H recursively starting with a single-faced  decomposition (Y0 , C0 ) = (∅, {V (H)\
∂(H)}). Let us define the measure µ(Y, C) = |Y | + C∈C |NH (C)|2 . We have µ(Y0 , C0 ) = |∂(H)|2 .
                                                      P
We are going to refine the single-faced decomposition until all the boundaried graphs HhCi, for
C ∈ C, are 8-inseparable, in each step decreasing the measure µ.
    Let (Yi , Ci ) be a single-faced decomposition of H. We use Lemma 7.20 with c = 4 to either
conclude that all HhCi, for C ∈ C, are 8-inseparable (then the procedure terminates) or to find a
canonical 4-separation (T1 , T2 , S) with respect to some single-faced embedding of HhCi for some
C ∈ C. Now we apply Lemma 7.22 to HhCi and (T1 , T2 , S). We obtain sets V1 , V2 ⊆ C \ S,
which form a partition of C \ S and satisfy NH (Vi ) ⊆ NH (C) ∪ S. Furthermore, each HhVi i
is single-faced. We define a new single-faced decomposition (Yi+1 , Ci+1 ), where Yi+1 = Yi ∪ S


                                                       53
and Ci+1 is obtained from Ci by replacing C with V1 and V2 . Lemma 7.22 guarantees that
|NH (V1 )|2 + |NH (V2 )|2 + |S| + 1 ≤ |NH (C)|2 , therefore µ(Yi+1 , Ci+1 ) ≤ µ(Yi , Ci ) − 1.
    In each step the measure µ(Yi , Ci ) decreases, so the algorithm terminates after at most µ(Y0 , C0 ) =
|∂(H)|2 steps. Let (Y` , C` ) be the final single-faced decomposition. We have |Y` | ≤ µ(Y` , C` ) ≤
µ(Y0 , C0 ) = |∂(H)|2 . For each C ∈ C` , the boundaried graph HhCi is single-faced (by the invariant
of a single-faced decomposition) and 8-inseparable (because the algorithm has terminated).

7.3    From inseparability to a solution preserver
In this section we show when GhAi is single-faced and c-inseparable then the empty set forms an
O(1)-preserver for A. In other words, for any solution S each vertex from S ∩ A can be replaced by
O(1) vertices in NG (A). First we need several observation about balls in the metric given by the
weak radial distance. Recall that for a plane graph G and u, v ∈ V (G) the weak radial distance
wG (u, v) is one less than the minimum length of a sequence of vertices that starts at u, ends in v,
and in which every two consecutive vertices either share an edge or lie on a common interior face.
Recall also that WG ` (X) denotes the ball of weak radial distance at most ` around vertex set X (see

Figure 5 for an illustration). The reason why we consider the weak radial distance instead of just
the radial distance (allowing to make paths also through the outer face) becomes evident in the
following proof.

Lemma 7.24. Let H be a single-faced boundaried plane graph and let X ⊆ V (H). Suppose that
T1 , T2 ⊆ ∂(H) are non-crossing. If WH ` (X) is an unrestricted (T , T )-separator, then there exists
                                                                    1 2
X 0 ⊆ WH ` (X) such that X 0 is an unrestricted (T , T )-separator and |X 0 | ≤ (2` + 1) · |X|.
                                                  1 2

Proof. Let H 00 be obtained from H by adding a new vertex t1 adjacent to all t in T1 and a new vertex
t2 adjacent to all t in T2 . Since T1 , T2 are non-crossing, t1 , t2 can be inserted without modifying the
embedding of H, within the outer face. Let us abbreviate X i = WH          i (X). By the assumption, X ` is

a (T1 , T2 )-separator in H .00

    Consider a directed graph R with vertex set X ` defined as follows. For each x ∈ X i \ X i−1 ,
i ∈ [`], we add a single edge from x to any vertex y ∈ X i−1 such that x, y share an interior face or
xy ∈ E(H). Such vertex y always exists by the definition of the metric wH . Since any x ∈ (X ` \ X)
has out-degree 1 in R, we can define a function r : (X ` \ X) → X ` given by the arcs of R.
    Let X 0 ⊆ X ` be defined by the following procedure. Initialize X 0 = X ` and while there exists
x ∈ X 0 \ X of in-degree 0 in R, such that X 0 \ x is still a (T1 , T2 )-separator in H 00 , remove x from X 0
and from R. Let C1 , C2 be the connected components in H 00 − X 0 containing T1 and T2 , respectively.
    We shall prove that |X 0 | ≤ (2` + 1) · |X|. Let Rx ⊆ X 0 be the set of vertices y ∈ X 0 admitting
a path y → x in R, including x. Fix x ∈ X and suppose that there are 3 vertices x1 , x2 , x3 ∈ Rx ,
each of in-degree 0 in R. Since none of these 3 vertices were removed during construction of X 0 ,
each of them is connected to both C1 and C2 in H 00 . Let H0 be obtained from H 00 be adding a new
vertex uf for each interior face of H and connecting uf to all vertices lying on this face. Recall
that H 00 was obtained by adding two vertices inside the outer face, so they do not interfere with
inserting vertices within interior faces. Therefore, H0 is still a planar graph. Let Y ⊆ V (H0 ) be
obtained from Rx \ {x1 , x2 , x2 } by adding all the adjacent face-vertices. Since x and r(x) either
share an edge or an interior face, the graph H0 [Y ] is connected. The vertices x1 , x2 , x3 and the sets
Y, C1 , C2 form branch sets of a K3,3 minor in H0 , which is a contradiction to the assumption that
such vertices x1 , x2 , x3 exist.
    We infer that Rx can contain at most 2 vertices of in-degree 0 in R, and because the out-degree
in R is at most 1, we obtain that |Rx ∩ X i | ≤ 2 for i ∈ [`] and so |Rx | ≤ 2` + 1. Since the sets Rx
sum up to X 0 , it follows that |X 0 | ≤ (2` + 1) · |X|.

                                                     54
    Lemma 7.24 implies that for a set X and integer `, the set WH ` (X) behaves similarly as a set of

size (2` + 1) · |X| with respect to separating non-crossing subsets of the boundary. The following
lemma deepens this analogy: we show that in a c-inseparable boundaried plane graph H, if the
number of boundary vertices is large compared to the size of some set X and integer `, then there
must be unique component of H − WH      ` (X) that contains almost all boundary vertices and all the

other components have at most O(c · ` · d) of them.
Lemma 7.25. Let H be a c-inseparable single-faced r-boundaried plane graph, ` be a positive integer,
and let X ⊆ V (H) be of size d. Suppose that 3c · (2` + 1) · d ≤ r. Then there is a single connected
component in H − WH ` (X) containing at least r − c · (2` + 1) · d vertices from ∂(H).

by the single-faced embedding. Let us choose an arbitrary element t1 ∈ ∂(H) and fix the ordering
t1 , . . . , tr of ∂(H) to start at this element.

    When GhAi is single-faced and c-inseparable, then for a solution S in G we consider the set
 3
WGhAi (S ∩ A) after fixing some single-faced embedding of GhAi. This will allow us to construct 3
connected separators between any vertex x ∈ S ∩ A and the large component from Lemma 7.25 in
order to argue for their irrelevance in the solution. To construct such separators we rely on the
following proposition. A plane triangulation is a plane graph with every face being incident to
exactly three vertices. Recall that when using the word separator without any adjective, we refer to
a restricted separator that does not intersect the elements being separated.
Proposition 7.26 ([MT01, Prop. 8.2.3]). Let u and v be distinct vertices in a plane triangulation
G. If S is a minimal (u, v)-separator, then G[S] is an induced, non-facial cycle.
   We remark that this result has been originally formulated for sets whose removal disconnect the
graph but the same argument holds for minimal (u, v)-separators.
Lemma 7.27. Let G be a connected plane graph, x ∈ X ⊆ V (G), and v ∈ V (G) \ WG         ` (X) for
                                                   `
some ` ∈ N. Then there exists either a vertex y ∈ WG (X) \ X which separates v from x or ` vertex-
                     ` (X) \ X forming a sequence of nested (v, x)-separators. In the latter case,
disjoint subsets of WG

                                                     55
each separator is inclusion-wise minimal and either induces a cycle or a path with both endpoints on
the outer face.
Proof. Consider the block-cut tree of G and the sequence of vertices u1 , u2 , . . . , um , where u1 = x,
um = v, and u2 , . . . , um−1 are the articulation points between x and v in G. If x, v belong to the
same biconnected component, then m = 2. More precisely, {ui } is a (v, x)-separator for i ∈ [2, m − 1]
and there are no more singleton (v, x)-separators in G apart from those in the sequence. Let j be
the highest index such that uj ∈ X. Note that j is well defined, as u1 = x ∈ X, and furthermore
j ≤ m − 1. If uj+1 ∈ WG    ` (X), then in particular u    6 v and we can set y = uj+1 to satisfy the
                                                      j+1 =
claim. Otherwise, let B ⊆ V (G) be the biconnected component of G containing uj and uj+1 . If
suffices to show that the claim holds for x0 = uj ∈ X and v 0 = uj+1 6∈ WG  ` (X), therefore we have

reduced the problem to the case when x, v lie in a single biconnected component B of G. Let us
focus on this case from now on.
    Let the graph G0B be obtained from B by
   1. adding a new vertex uf in the center of each face f in B,
   2. for each new vertex uf , connecting it to all the vertices from B lying on the face f .
Let us note several properties of G0B . Let the set U0 consist of the new single vertex put in place of
the outer face of G, if that vertex belongs to V (G0B ), or be empty if the outer face is not adjacent
to any vertex of B.
   1. If u, v ∈ V (B) and uv ∈ E(G0B ), then uv ∈ E(G).
   2. If S ⊆ V (B) is a (v, x)-separator in G0B − U0 , then S is a (v, x)-separator in G.
   3. Since B is a biconnected component of G, each face of B forms a cycle and thus G0B is a plane
      triangulation.
   4. Let Sj = {u ∈ V (B) | wG (X, u) = j}. Then Sj ∪ U0 is a (v, x)-separator in G0B for j ∈ [`].
      The last property requires a short justification. Suppose there exists a (v, x)-path P 0 in
G0B − (Sj ∪ U0 ) and let P be the sequence of vertices from V (B) occurring on P 0 . Since P 0 does
not use the vertex corresponding to the outer face, each pair of consecutive vertices yi , yj from P
share an interior face, thus |wG (X, yi ) − wG (X, yj )| ≤ 1. Since wG (X, x) = 0 and wG (X, v) > ` ≥ j,
P must contain a vertex from Sj ; a contradiction.
      We shall now show that for each j ∈ [`] there exists a set Sj0 ⊆ Sj such that G[Sj0 ] is connected
and Sj0 ∪ U0 is a (v, x)-separator in G0B of one of the two desired forms. Let Sj0 be an inclusion-wise
minimal subset of Sj0 ∪ U0 which is still a (v, x)-separator in G0B . Since G0B is a plane triangulation,
Proposition 7.26 applies and we get that G0B [Sj0 ] is an induced cycle. If Sj0 ∩ U0 = ∅, then Sj0 induces
a cycle in G. Otherwise, recall that U0 contains at most one element representing the outer face.
Removing a single vertex from a cycle turns it into a path, hence G0B [Sj0 \ U0 ] = G[Sj0 \ U0 ] induces
a path. Since the vertex in U0 was placed in the outer face, the path has both endpoints on the
outer face of G. Note that Sj0 = Sj0 \ U0 is a (v, x) separator in G0B − U0 . Because Sj0 ⊆ Sj ⊆ V (B),
we obtain that Sj0 is the desired (v, x)-separator in B, and thus in G.
      These separators are clearly vertex-disjoint and contained in WG          ` (X) \ X. The fact that they

can be ordered into a nested sequence follows from their connectivity and minimality. For i =             6 j,
the set Si0 must be fully contained in some component of G − Sj0 , precisely either in RG (x, Sj0 ) or
in RG (v, Sj0 ). From minimality we get that each vertex of Sj0 is adjacent to both RG (x, Sj0 ) and
RG (v, Sj0 ). In the first case Si0 must separate x from Sj0 and the second case it must separate v from
Sj0 . Therefore, either RG (x, Si0 ) ⊆ RG (x, Sj0 ) or RG (x, Sj0 ) ⊆ RG (x, Si0 ).

                                                     56
    Note that even though the proof gives a natural order of separators (Si0 )`i=1 , they might be
nested in a different order. This is because we require Si0 ⊆ {u ∈ B | wG (X, u) = i}, not
Si0 ⊆ {u ∈ B | wG (x, u) = i}. This is necessary to guarantee that Si0 ∩ X = ∅, which will be an
important property in the proof of Lemma 7.29.
    When given a single-faced embedding of a c-inseparable graph GhAi and a solution S in G, we
are going to remove S ∩ A from the solution and replace it with some subset of NG (A), of size
comparable to |S ∩ A|. As the new solution removes some vertices from NG (A) we need to ensure
that this cannot disconnect the separators constructed in Lemma 7.27.

Lemma 7.28. Let G be a connected plane graph, u, v ∈ V (G), and S ⊆ V (G) be a minimal
(u, v)-separator such that G[S] either induces a cycle or a path with both endpoints on the outer face.
If Y ⊆ V (G) \ {u, v} is a subset of vertices lying on the outer face of G and S 6⊆ Y , then S \ Y is a
connected (u, v)-separator in G − Y .

Proof. It is clear that S \ Y is a (u, v)-separator in G − Y and we only need to show that it is
connected. We will rely on the observation that, since S is minimal, each vertex s ∈ S is adjacent
to both RG (u, S) and RG (v, S). Let G0 be obtained from G by adding a new vertex f adjacent to
all vertices in Y . Since they lie on the outer face of G, the graph G0 is planar.
     Suppose that S induces a cycle and that S \ Y is not connected. Then there exist vertices
y1 , y2 ∈ Y ∩ S and a partition of S \ {y1 , y2 } into connected vertex sets S1 , S2 so that each edge
set EG (yi , Sj ) for i, j ∈ {1, 2} is non-empty. The sets RG (u, S), S1 ∪ RG (v, S), S2 , {y1 , f }, {y2 } form
branch sets of a K5 -minor model in G0 , which gives a contradiction.
     Now suppose that S induces a path with both endpoints on the outer face and that S \ Y
is not connected. Then there exists vertex y ∈ Y ∩ S and a partition of S \ {y} into connected
vertex sets S1 , S2 so that each of them contains a vertex lying on the outer face. The sets
RG (u, S), RG (v, S), {f }, S1 , S2 , {y} form branch sets of a K3,3 -minor model in G0 , which again leads
to a contradiction.

    We are ready to show that when GhAi is single-faced and 8-inseparable then ∅ is an α-preserver
for A, where α = 3 · 8 · 7 = 168. We first fix a single-faced embedding of GhAi. When S is a solution
in G and X = S ∩ A, we consider terminal pairs separated by WGhAi    3    (X) and use Lemma 7.25 to
identify a subset of NG (A) of size comparable to |X|, which intersects all the separated terminal
pairs. We can add it to S, creating a larger solution S 0 (see Figure 5). We need to argue that
vertices from X can be now removed from S 0 without invalidating the solution. To this end, we
use Lemma 7.27 to construct 3 nested connected separators between any x ∈ X and NG (A) \ S 0 in
G − (S 0 \ {x}) which allows us to use the planarity criterion from Lemma 3.17.

Lemma 7.29. Let A ⊆ V (G) be such that GhAi is an 8-inseparable single-faced boundaried
planar graph. Then for any planar modulator S ⊆ V (G), there exists a planar modulator S 0 ⊆
(S \ A) ∪ NG (A), such that S 0 \ NG [A] = S \ NG [A] and |S 0 | ≤ |S| + 168 · |S ∩ A|.

Proof. Let r = |NG (A)|, X = S ∩ A, and d = |X|. If 3 · 8 · 7 · d > r, then we set S 0 = (S \ A) ∪ NG (A).
Since the graph G[A] is planar, G − (A ∪ S 0 ) is a subgraph of G − S, and G − S 0 is a disjoint union
of these graphs, we get that S 0 is also a planar modulator of the desired size. (This simple case
actually forms the worst-case scenario for the size of S 0 .)
    Suppose now that 3 · 8 · 7 · d ≤ r. Note that all boundary vertices are connected in GhAi due to
8-inseparability and, by the definition of being single-faced (Definition 3.12), GhAi is connected.
Let us fix any single-faced embedding of GhAi, so the set WGhAi     3   (X) is well-defined. We apply
Lemma 7.25 to GhAi and X = S ∩ A, with c = 8 and ` = 3, to infer that there exists a single

   We have shown that when GhAi is single-faced and 8-inseparable then any solution to G can be
made disjoint from A with a small cost. This finishes the proof of Proposition 7.13 (restated below)
and the construction of solution preservers for single-faced boundaried graphs.

Proposition 7.13. Let A ⊆ V (G) be such that GhAi is a single-faced boundaried graph. Then
there exists a set SA ⊆ A of size at most |NG (A)|2 which is a 168-preserver for A. Furthermore, SA
can be computed in polynomial time.

Proof. We apply Lemma 7.23 to compute, in polynomial time, a boundaried decomposition (Y, C) of
GhAi, so that |Y | ≤ |NG (A)|2 and for each C ∈ C it holds that GhCi is single-faced and 8-inseparable.
By Lemma 7.29, the empty set forms a 168-preserver for each C ∈ C. Hence, Lemma 7.7 implies
that Y is a 168-preserver for A.


8     Compressing the negligible components
After collecting the solution preservers, we obtain a planar modulator X 0 ⊆ G with the following
property: there exists a 170-approximate solution S 0 such that S 0 ⊆ X 0 . In the final step of the
algorithm we want to compress each connected component C of G − X 0 ; these component are called
negligible. By Lemma 7.11 we can augment the solution preservers computed for boundaried plane
graphs with bounded radial diameter, so that the negligible components can be represented with
boundaried plane graphs which are circumscribed. Recall that in a circumscribed boundaried plane
graph H the image of each vertex in ∂(H) belongs to the outer face of H − ∂(H). We refer to the
set of vertices of H − ∂(H) lying on the outer face as ∆(H) ⊆ V (H) \ ∂(H); equivalently, this is
the first outerplanarity layer of H − ∂(H). We are going to decompose H into cycles and bridges,
making it easier to analyze H through the criterion from Lemma 3.15. Refer to Figure 6 for an
illustration of the following concept.

Definition 8.1. A bridge decomposition of a circumscribed r-boundaried plane graph H is a pair
(Y, B) satisfying the following conditions:

    1. Y ⊆ ∆(H) and |Y | ≤ 24 · r,

    2. B is a subfamily of the connected components of H − (∂(H) ∪ ∆(H)) with |B| ≤ 24 · r,


                                                    59
Figure 6: Schematic illustration of a bridge decomposition of a circumscribed 9-boundaried graph H.
The boundary ∂(H) is drawn as red squares. The vertices of ∆(H) are shown by circles. Each
connected component of H − (∂(H) ∪ ∆(H)) is a shaded region. The set Y consists of the blue
circles and the set B of the single component inside the blue curve. After removing the blue and red
objects, the neighborhoods of the resulting components are bounded.

                                                      S
   3. each connected component of H − (∂(H) ∪ Y ∪ A∈B V (A)) has at most 2 neighbors in ∂(H),
      at most 10 neighbors in Y , and is adjacent to at most 10 components from B.

    Observe that if we only could assume
                                  S      that the subgraphs from B have constant size, then we
could mark all the vertices in Y ∪ B∈B V (B), and the leftover components would have only O(1)
neighbors each, making them amenable to protrusion replacement. However these subgraphs may
be large and we will need to compress them.
    First we show that a bridge decomposition can be easily computed with known techniques for
processing bounded-treewidth subgraphs.

Lemma 8.2. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a circumscribed r-boundaried plane
graph H, returns a bridge decomposition of H.

Proof. Let B0 denote the family of all connected components of H − (∂(H) ∪ ∆(H)). Consider a
circumscribed r-boundaried plane graph H 0 obtained from H by contracting each component from
B0 into a single vertex. It is clear that this operation does not affect the boundary and the outer
face, so we can write ∂(H 0 ) = ∂(H) and ∆(H 0 ) = ∆(H). Let us refer to the set of vertices obtained
by contracting components from B0 as B 0 ⊆ V (H 0 ). We have a natural mapping φB : B 0 → B0 which
maps vertices of B 0 to their pre-image before contraction. The graph H 0 − ∂(H 0 ) is 2-outerplanar
(see Definition 6.7) therefore its treewidth is at most 5 [Bod98]. A corresponding tree decomposition
of width 5 can be found in polynomial time [Bod96].
    We apply Lemma 3.7 to H 0 with S = ∂(H 0 ) and η = 5 to mark a set Y 0 ⊆ V (H 0 ) \ ∂(H 0 ) such
that |Y 0 | ≤ 24 · r and each connected component of H 0 − (∂(H 0 ) ∪ Y 0 ) has at most 2 neighbors in
∂(H 0 ) and at most 10 neighbors in Y 0 . Let Y = Y 0 ∩ ∆(H 0 ) be the set of marked vertices on the
outer face, B = {φB (b) | b ∈ Y 0 ∩ B 0 } be the set of connected components
                                                                        S     from B0 that got marked
after contraction. Each connected component of H − (∂(H) ∪ Y ∪ B∈B V (B)) corresponds to a


                                                 60
connected component of H 0 − (∂(H 0 ) ∪ Y 0 ) and its neighborhood is the union of the corresponding
branch sets. The claim follows.

    The next lemma explains the correspondence between a bridge decomposition and the concept
of a C-bridge. The assumption that H is circumscribed plays an important role here. Recall the
notion of overlap graph O from Lemma 3.15.

Lemma 8.3. Let (Y, B) be a bridge decomposition of a circumscribed boundaried plane graph H
and B ∈ B. Then there exists a cycle C such that V (C) ⊆ ∆(H) and B is a connected component
of H − V (C). Furthermore, the graph O(H − ∂(H), C) has no edges, that is, no two C-bridges in
H − ∂(H) overlap.

Proof. Consider a circumscribed boundaried plane graph H 0 obtained from H by contracting each
component of H − (∂(H) ∪ ∆(H)) into a single vertex. Let b be the vertex given by contracting B.
Since B ∩ ∆(H) = ∅, vertex b does not lie on the outer face of H 0 . Hence the image of b lies in an
interior face f of H[∆(H)] consisting of vertices which lie on the outer face of H, which implies
that the boundary cycle of f is a simple cycle C with V (C) ⊆ ∆(H). We proceed to show that C
has the desired properties.
    Observe that NH 0 (b) ⊆ V (C): by planarity of the embedding, b cannot have neighbors on the
exterior of C, while b cannot have neighbors embedded in the proper interior of C as they would
have been part of the same connected component of H − (∂(H) ∪ ∆(H)) and therefore would have
been contracted when forming b. Hence NH 0 (b) ⊆ V (C) which implies NH (B) ⊆ V (C). Since H[B]
is connected, this implies B is a connected component of H − V (C). It remains to prove that no
two C-bridges in H − ∂(H) overlap.
    Note that for each connected component A of H − V (C) that is embedded on the exterior
of C, we have |NG (A) ∩ V (C)| ≤ 2: if A had at least three neighbors on the cycle C, then one of
these neighbors does not lie on the outer face of H since it is enclosed by A and a path around
the cycle. The same argument shows that if |NG (A) ∩ V (C)| = 2, then the two neighbors of A
on C are consecutive along C. Note that by definition, such a C-bridge does not overlap any other
C-bridges. This holds also when |NG (A) ∩ V (C)| = 1. Finally, no C-bridge that is a chord of C can
be embedded on the exterior of C, as it would again separate a vertex of C from the outer face.
    When it comes to C-bridges corresponding to connected components of H − V (C) or chords of C
embedded on the interior of C, by Observation 3.14 any two C-bridges which overlap are embedded
on opposite sides of C, so there are no overlaps between C-bridges embedded in the interior. The
claim follows.

    Let G be a graph, D ⊆ V (G), and consider the embedding of GhDi as a circumscribed boundaried
plane graph H. If A ∈ B is a C-bridge for some cycle C within ∆(H), then any planar modulator
S in G which is disjoint from D, remains valid if we replace A with an equivalent C-bridge, that
is, having the same attachments at C and not violating planarity of A ∪ C (see Lemma 3.15).
This holds in particular for a bridge obtained by contracting A into a single vertex. However such
a modification is insufficient to provide the backward safeness: for any solution of size at most
k (possibly intersecting D) we need to be able to lift it back. A possible “dirty” solution is to
mark such vertices as undeletable. This however would lead to producing an instance of a different
problem (which could be harder to approximate) and would by insufficient to obtain Theorem 1.2.
Instead, we will replace A with a well of depth O(k) and perimeter k O(1) . We perform this operation
in several steps. First we introduce the concept of drawing cycles within C surrounding the vertices
from A.



                                                 61
       Figure 7: A result of 3-nesting the vertex set given by the five vertices in the center.


Definition 8.4. Let A be a vertex subset of a plane graph G, so that the induced embedding of
GhAi is circumscribed. We say that A is c-well-nested in G if the following conditions hold:

   1. the first c outerplanarity layers of G[A] are cycles of fixed length m ≥ 3, the i-th outer cycle
      comprising vertices v1i , . . . , vm
                                         i , ordered clockwise,


   2. for each j ∈ [m], there is at most one vertex u ∈ NG (A) such that uvj1 ∈ E(G),

   3. vji is adjacent to vji+1 for all i ∈ [c − 1], j ∈ [m].

Definition 8.5. Let A be a vertex subset of a (boundaried) plane graph G, so that the induced
embedding of GhAi is circumscribed and EG (A, V (G) \ A) ≥ 3. The act of c-nesting the set A means
replacing G by G0 given by

   1. enumerating the edges in EG (A, V (G) \ A) in clockwise order with respect to the embedding of
      GhAi; let us refer to them as e1 , e2 , . . . , em ,

   2. subdividing each edge ei c times, creating vertices vi1 , vi2 , . . . , vic , counting towards the endpoint
      in A,

   3. connecting vertices vij and vi+1
                                   j                                         j
                                       for j ∈ [c], i ∈ [m − 1], as well as vm and v1j for each j ∈ [c],

and replacing A with A0 being the union of A and all the new vertices. See Figure 7. We treat the
remaining vertices and the corresponding points in the embedding as unaffected, that is, V (G0 ) \ A0 =
V (G) \ A.

    The operation of c-nesting is clearly backward safe as the original graph is a minor of the graph
after the modification. We take note of several other useful properties of c-nesting.

Observation 8.6. Let H be a circumscribed boundaried plane graph with a bridge decomposition
(Y, B) and A ∈ B. Suppose that H 0 and A0 have been obtained by c-nesting the set A. Then the
following hold.

   1. H 0 is a circumscribed boundaried plane graph,

   2. (Y, B 0 ) is a bridge decomposition of H 0 , where B 0 is given by replacing A with A0 in B,

   3. the set A0 is c-well-nested in H 0 ,


                                                       62
  4. the size of each of the c outermost layers in H 0 [A0 ] equals the number of edges in EH 0 (A0 , V (H 0 )\
     A0 ), which is the same as the number of edges in EH (A, V (H) \ A),

  5. H can be obtained back from H 0 by removing or contracting edges which are non-incident to
     the boundary ∂(H) = ∂(H 0 ).

   We now prove the forward safeness of c-nesting with respect to solutions disjoint from the
subgraph being decomposed.

Lemma 8.7. Consider a graph G, vertex set D ⊆ V (G), so that GhDi admits a circumscribed
embedding, and let H = GhDi denote the corresponding circumscribed boundaried plane graph. Let
(Y, B) be a bridge decomposition of H and A ∈ B. Suppose that (H 0 , A0 ) has been obtained by
c-nesting the set A in H and let G0 = G∂ [D] ⊕ H 0 . If S ⊆ V (G) \ D is a planar modulator in G,
then it is a planar modulator in G0 .

Proof. By Lemma 8.3 there exists a cycle C such that V (C) ⊆ ∆(H) ⊆ D and A is a connected
component of H − V (C). Since S ∩ D = ∅, A is also a connected component of (G − S) − V (C).
We apply Lemma 3.15 with respect to G − S and C. The overlap graph O(G − S, C) is not affected
by c-nesting the set A, and the only C-bridge that has been modified is the one generated by
component A of (G − S) − V (C), which is replaced by A0 . The graph induced by A0 ∪ V (C) is a
subgraph of H 0 , hence it is planar. Therefore S is still a planar modulator in G∂ [D] ⊕ H 0 .

    After c-nesting some set of vertices A for c = Ω(k), they become surrounded by Ω(k) nested
cyclic A-planarizing separators. This suffices to make each vertex in A k-irrelevant. We would like
now to remove the vertex set A but the number of vertices introduced during c-nesting might still
be large if the number of edges outgoing from A was large. In order to handle this, we first state a
simple criterion for proving that some separator planarizes a vertex.

Lemma 8.8. Let G be a graph, A ⊆ V (G), S ⊆ A, and v ∈ A \ S. If RGhAi (v, S) ∩ NG (A) = ∅
then RGhAi (v, S) = RG (v, S).

Proof. Assume the contrary: then there exists a path P in G − S that starts at v and ends
at u 6∈ RGhAi (v, S). The path P cannot be fully contained in A because then we would have
u ∈ RGhAi (v, S). Therefore the path P intersects NG (A): let P 0 be the subpath of P starting at v
and ending with the first occurrence of a vertex x from V (P ) ∩ NG (A). Then P 0 witnesses that
x ∈ RGhAi (v, S) but this contradicts the assumption that RGhAi (v, S) ∩ NG (A) = ∅. The claim
follows.

    The next lemma states then when the set A ∈ B is (k + 5)-nested in GhAi and the number
of edges outgoing from A is large compared to k and |NG (A)|, then there exists an edge which is
k-irrelevant. Recall the notion of a pseudo-nested sequence from Definition 3.20. In the following
proof, we slightly abuse the notation and write RG (v, P ) to denote RG (v, V (P )) when P is a
subgraph of G (a path or a cycle).

Lemma 8.9. Let G be a graph, k be an integer, and D ⊆ V (G) with |NG (D)| = r. Suppose
that GhDi has a circumscribed embedding and let (Y, B) be a bridge decomposition of GhDi with
respect to this embedding. Suppose that A ∈ B is (k + 5)-well-nested and satisfies |EG (A, NG (A))| >
2 · r2 · (2k + 7)3 . Then there exist vertices v ∈ ∂G (A), u ∈ NG (A), such that uv ∈ E(G) and one of
the following holds:

  1. there exists a sequence of k + 3 nested v-planarizing connected vertex sets in G, or


                                                     63
   2. there exists a sequence of k + 3 pseudo-nested v-planarizing cycles in G.

Furthermore, such a pair (u, v) can be found in polynomial time.

Proof. Let Ai be the i-th outermost cycle in G[A], where i ∈ [k + 5], and let m denote the length of
each cycle Ai = (v1i , . . . , vm
                                i ). Let C be a cycle for which N (A) ⊆ V (C) ⊆ D and A is a connected
                                                                          G
component of GhDi − V (C) and therefore of G − V (C), guaranteed to exist by Lemma 8.3. We
shall consider three scenarios. We refer to Figure 8 for an illustration.
     First, suppose there exists a vertex u ∈ NG (A) such that |NG (u) ∩ A| ≥ 2k + 7. Since the
vertices from NG (u) ∩ A lie on the cycle A1 , we can choose an integer sequence (j1 , j2 , . . . , j2k+7 )
so that (1) for i ∈ [2k + 7] we have ji ∈ [m] and uvj1i ∈ E(G), (2) the vertices vj11 , vj12 , . . . , vj12k+7
lie in this order on the cycle A1 , and (3) the edges uvj11 , uvj12k+7 separate the exterior of cycle A1
into a bounded region containing all the remaining edges uvj12 , . . . uvj12k+6 and an unbounded region
containing all vertices from NG (D) (see Figure 8 on the left, where the edges uvj11 , . . . , uvj12k+7 are
the edges between u and the blue cycle A1 , from left to right).
     Let v = vj1k+4 . Intuitively, a cycle Ci consists of two paths starting in u: the two paths respectively
go to the vertices i steps left and right of v, move inwards over i + 1 cycles of the well, connecting
to each other via a subpath of the cycle Ai+1 . More precisely, for i ∈ [k + 3] we set h(i, 1) = jk+4−i
and h(i, 2) = jk+4+i to denote the two indices that are in distance i from the middle. We define a
                                              i+1                                                            i+1
path Pi1 as (u, vh(i,1)
                   1        2
                         , vh(i,1) , . . . , vh(i,1) ) and similarly Pi2 as (u, vh(i,2)
                                                                                 1         2
                                                                                        , vh(i,2) , . . . , vh(i,2) ). We define
                                                                                i+1                i+1
a cycle Ci as a concatentaion of paths Pi1 , Pi2 and a path that connects vh(i,1)         and vh(i,2)    within the
cycle Ai+1 ; there are two choices for the latter path and we choose the one that, together with Pi1
and Pi2 , separates v from V (Ai+2 ).
     We show that (C1 , . . . , Ck+3 ) is a sequence of pseudo-nested v-planarizing cycles. For each
i ∈ [k + 3], the cycle Ci in the plane graph GhDi separates the plane into two regions: one contains
v and the other one V (Ai+2 ) and NG (D). By Lemma 8.8 we get that RG (v, Ci ) = RGhDi (v, Ci ) ⊆ A.
This implies that RG [v, Ci ] induces a planar graph. Furthermore, let 1 ≤ i < j ≤ k + 3. By the
construction, V (Ci ) ∩ V (Cj ) = {u}. The cycle Ci separates v from V (Cj ) \ {u} in GhDi. By the
same argument as above, we obtain that RG (v, Ci ) ∩ V (Cj ) = ∅.
     Before analyzing the second and third scenario, let us greedily construct a family of paths P,
which is initialized as empty. As long as we can find a path in GhDi connecting A to NG (D) which
is internally vertex-disjoint from the already constructed paths, we choose a shortest path with this
property and insert it to P. Such a family can be easily constructed in polynomial time. Recall
that A1 is the set of vertices on the outer face of G[A], i.e., its first outerplanarity layer. By the
definition of a c-well-nested set, each vertex x ∈ A can have at most one neighbor in NG (A), and it
is possible only when x ∈ A1 . Because we always choose a shortest path, which does not contain
vertices from A in its interior, the A-endpoints of the paths in P are therefore distinct and belong
to A1 . The usage of shortest paths also implies that (*) the interior of each path P ∈ P does not
contain any vertices from NG (D) and (**) the set V (P ) contains exactly one vertex from NG (A).
     As the second scenario we consider the case |P| > r2 · (2k + 7). As |NG (D)| = r, this implies
that for some t ∈ NG (D) there exists a subfamily Pt ⊆ P of ` > r · (2k + 7) internally vertex-disjoint
(A, t)-paths in GhDi. Since the A-endpoints of these paths are distinct and lie on the cycle A1 ,
we can choose an ordering (P1 , . . . , P` ) of Pt and an integer sequence (j1 , j2 , . . . , j` ) so that (1) for
i ∈ [`] we have ji ∈ [m] and Pi is a (t, vj1i )-path, (2) the vertices vj11 , vj12 , . . . , vj1` lie in this order
on the cycle A1 , and (3) the paths P1 , P` separate the exterior of cycle A1 into a bounded region
containing all the remaining paths P2 , . . . P`−1 and an unbounded region. Since A1 is a cycle, we
can construct regions R1 , . . . , R`−1 , so that Ri is the bounded subset of the plane surrounded by Pi ,

    Let v = vj1` . We show that (C1 , . . . , Ck+3 ) is a sequence of pseudo-nested v-planarizing cycles.

    We want to show that for each i ∈ [k + 3] it holds that RGhDi (v, Pi ) ∩ NG (D) = ∅. Suppose
otherwise, that there exists a path P v in GhDi − V (Pi ) which connects v to NG (D). This path must
intersect the interior of some path P 0 ∈ P because of the greedy construction. The set V (P 0 ) ∩ V (C)
contains a vertex from outside S. Therefore the set V (P v ) ∪ V (P 0 ) contains a path P 00 connecting
v to V (C) \ S in GhDi − V (Pi ). Let us consider the intersection of V (P 00 ) with V (C). First, V (P 00 )
must intersect the subsegment of S between uk+4−i and uk+4+i (exclusively) by the construction
of Pi ; let us refer to this subsegment as Si . Hence, there is a subpath of P 00 which connects Si to
V (C) \ (Pi ∪ Si ), internally disjoint from V (C). The interior of this subpath must be contained
in some C-bridge B other than the one generated by the component A of G − V (C). However,
as uk+4−i and uk+4+i are attachments of the bridge generated by the connected component A
of G − V (C), the bridges A and B overlap. This is impossible due to Lemma 8.3.
    We have shown that RGhDi (v, Pi ) ∩ NG (D) = ∅, so by Lemma 8.8 we get that RG (v, Pi ) =
RGhDi (v, Pi ) and so RG [v, Pi ] induces a planar graph. Let i < j ≤ k + 3. By the construction,
V (Pi ) and V (Pj ) are disjoint. To see that RGhDi (v, Pi ) ∩ V (Pj ) = ∅, observe that V (Pj ) belongs to
the same component of GhDi − V (Pi ) as V (Ai+2 ). By the construction, this component does not
contain v.
    We have thus proven that there always exist a vertex v, with a neighbor in NG (A), that satisfies
the conditions of the lemma. The proof relies on degree counting and computing shortest paths,
therefore it can be turned into a polynomial-time algorithm.

    We are ready to prove the main lemma of this section. Given the boundaried graph GhDi with
a circumscribed embedding, we compute a bridge decomposition (Y, B) to identify O(r) subgraphs
that need to compressed. For A ∈ B, we first perform c-nesting for c = k + 5, to make it amenable
to Lemma 8.9. This allows us to reduce the number of edges outgoing from A. Next, we perform
c-nesting again (this time it suffices to take c = k + 3) to surround all the vertices in A with k + 3
cycles of length k O(1) and remove vertices from A due to their k-irrelevance. The properties of
the bridge decomposition guarantees that marking all the vertices in Y and the union of closed
neighborhoods from B (in total k O(1) vertices now) yields a decomposition into subgraphs of constant
neighborhood size, which can be reduced by protrusion replacement.
Lemma 8.10. Let G be a graph, k be an integer, and D ⊆ V (G) with |NG (D)| = r. Suppose that
H = GhDi admits a circumscribed embedding. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
G, D, k, and a circumscribed embedding of H, outputs a labeled r-boundaried planar graph H 0 , and
a vertex set F 0 ⊆ V (H 0 ) \ ∂(H 0 ) such that the following conditions hold.
   1. each connected component of H 0 − (∂(H 0 ) ∪ F 0 ) has at most 32 neighbors,
   2. |F 0 | ≤ 50 · r3 · (2k + 7)4 ,

                                                                 66
   3. if S ⊆ V (G) \ D is a planar modulator in G, then it is a planar modulator in H 0 ⊕ G∂ [D].

   4. there is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given G, D, G0 = H 0 ⊕G∂ [D], and a planar modulator
      S 0 in G0 of size at most k, returns a planar modulator in G of size at most |S 0 |.
Proof. We begin by computing a bridge decomposition (Y, B) of GhDi (Lemma 8.2). For a set
A ∈ B with at least 3 edges in EH (A, V (H) \ A) we will perform a series of graph modifications
and analyze a series of tuples (Hi , Yi , Bi , Ai ), where Hi is a boundaried plane graph, (Yi , Bi ) is
a bridge decomposition of Hi , and Ai ∈ Bi . The lower bound on the number of outgoing edges
is necessary to perform c-nesting in a well-defined way. As a goal, we want to replace A with a
subgraph of moderate size and moderate neighborhood. There are two kinds of safeness we need
to ensure during the graph modification process. Forward safeness means that for every vertex
set S ⊆ V (G∂ [D]) if S is a planar modulator in Hi ⊕ G∂ [D], then it is also a planar modulator in
Hi+1 ⊕ G∂ [D]. Backward safeness means that any planar modulator S in Hi+1 ⊕ G∂ [D] of size at
most k can be turned, in polynomial time, into a planar modulator in Hi ⊕ G∂ [D] of size at most
|S|.
     Let α = 2 · r2 · (2k + 7)3 and A ∈ B be fixed. We have H1 = GhDi, (Y1 , B1 ) = (Y, B), and
A1 = A ∈ B. We obtain (H2 , Y2 , B2 , A2 ) by (k + 5)-nesting the set A1 . The forward safeness of this
modification follows directly from Lemma 8.7. The backward safeness holds because H1 ⊕ G∂ [D] is
a minor of H2 ⊕ G∂ [D] (Observation 8.6(5)). In the next step we reduce the number of outgoing
edges from A2 in H2 . As long as it holds that |EH2 (A2 , V (H2 ) \ A2 )| > α, we apply Lemma 8.9 to
find vertices v ∈ ∂H2 (A2 ), u ∈ NH2 (A2 ), such that uv ∈ E(H2 ) and one of the following holds:
   1. there exists a sequence of k + 3 nested v-planarizing connected vertex sets in H2 ⊕ G∂ [D], or

   2. there exists a sequence of k + 3 pseudo-nested v-planarizing cycles in H2 ⊕ G∂ [D].
In both cases the edge uv is k-irrelevant due to Lemmas 3.19 and 3.21, which implies backward
safeness of removing uv. The forward safeness of an edge removal is trivial.
      At some point we reach a situation where these reductions rules are no longer applicable. Let
(H3 , Y3 , B3 , A3 ) denote the graph and the decomposition after performing the edge deletions. We
have |EH3 (A3 , V (H3 ) \ A3 )| ≤ α, which implies that |NH3 (A3 )| ≤ α. We obtain (H4 , Y4 , B4 , A4 ) by
(k + 3)-nesting the set A3 . The forward and backward safeness again follow from Lemma 8.7 and
the minor relation. By Observation 8.6(4) we know that the size of each of the (k + 3) outermost
layers in H4 [A4 ] is at most α and |NH4 (A4 )| ≤ α. Consider now a vertex v ∈ A4 which does not
belong to any of the (k + 3) outermost layers in H4 [A4 ]. These layers form a sequence of k + 3
nested v-planarizing connected sets in H4 ⊕ G∂ [D], so v is k-irrelevant in H4 ⊕ G∂ [D]. We can thus
remove all such vertices and obtain the final tuple (H5 , Y5 , B5 , A5 ). The forward safeness is trivial
and the backward safeness follows from the definition of k-irrelevance. In the resulting graph we
have |A5 | ≤ (k + 3) · α and |NH5 (A5 )| ≤ α.
      With the recipe for compressing a single bridge component in B without affecting the other parts
of the graph, we can perform this replacement for every A ∈ B satisfying |EH (A, V (H) \ A)| ≥ 3.
Let (H 0 , Y 0 , B 0 ) be obtained from (GhDi, Y, B) by such a procedure and B30 ⊆ B 0 denote the
subfamily of sets with at least 3 outgoing edges, for which we have performed the modification.
We assign labels to ∂(H 0 ) to make them consistent with the (implicit) labeling of ∂(H). We set
G0 = H 0 ⊕ G∂ [D]. The properties of forward and backward safeness imply conditions (3) and
(4)—the existence of an algorithm that lifts solutions from G0 to G. Let F 0 be the union of NH 0 [A]
over all A ∈ B30 plus the union of NH 0 (A) over all A ∈ B 0 \ B30 plus Y 0 . For A ∈ B 0 \ B30 it holds
|NH 0 (A)| ≤ 2 and for A ∈ B 0 it holds |NH 0 [A]| ≤ (k + 4) · α. We have |B 0 | = |B|, |Y 0 | = |Y |, and so
|F 0 | ≤ |B 0 | · (k + 4) · α + |Y 0 | ≤ 24r · (2 · r2 · (2k + 7)4 + 1) ≤ 50 · r3 · (2k + 7)4 .

                                                     67
     Finally, we want to estimate the neighborhood size of connected components R0 of H 0 − (∂(H 0 ) ∪
F 0 ). If R0 ∈ B 0 \ B30 then clearly |NH 0 (R0 )| ≤ 2. Otherwise,
                                                            S        the vertex set V (R0 ) is a subset of V (R),
where R is some component of H − (∂(H) ∪ Y ∪ A∈B V (A)). Hence, by the properties of a bridge
decomposition, R can have at most 2 neighbors in ∂(H), at most 10 neighbors in Y , and can be
adjacent to at most 10 components from B. This means that R0 also can have at most 2 neighbors
in ∂(H 0 ) and at most 10 neighbors in Y 0 . To estimate the number of neighbors of R0 in NH 0 [A], for
A ∈ B 0 , first observe that these neighbors must belong to NH 0 (A). It remains to show that R0 can
be adjacent to at most 2 vertices from NH 0 (A) for a fixed A ∈ B 0 . Suppose otherwise, that there
are 3 such vertices v1 , v2 , v3 . Because (Y 0 , B 0 ) is a bridge decomposition of H 0 , they must lie on the
outer face of H 0 − ∂(H 0 ). We can thus add a vertex u to H 0 − ∂(H 0 ), adjacent to all v1 , v2 , v3 , while
maintaining planarity. However, v1 , v2 , v3 together with u, V (R0 ), V (A) form a minor model of K3,3 ;
a contradiction. In summary, the set NH 0 (R0 ) can comprise of at most 2 vertices from ∂(H 0 ), 10
vertices from Y 0 , and 2 · 10 vertices in total from NG0 [A], where A ∈ B 0 , which gives at most 32
neighbors of R0 .


9     Wrapping up
We are ready to combine all the intermediate results into a proof of the main theorem. Proposition 6.21
provides us with a strong modulator X of fragmentation k O(1) and a family of embeddings for
GhCi for C ∈ CC(G − X) of radial diameter k O(1) . We will now summarize Sections 7 and 8 with
Proposition 9.1. First we use Proposition 7.15 to compute a 170-preserver SC for C, and then
Lemma 7.11 to find a superset YC of SC so that NG (C)∪YC is radially connected in GhCi. The union
of sets YC together with X gives a modulator X 0 in G containing some 170-approximate solution.
Furthermore, for C 0 ∈ CC(G − X 0 ) we get a circumscribed embedding of GhC 0 i. Lemma 8.10 allows
us to mark k O(1) vertices in C 0 so that the remaining components have only O(1) neighbors. Such
components can be reduced to be of size O(1) using protrusion replacement.
