Theorem 9.2. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph G and an integer k, either
   1. outputs a planar modulator in G of size mvp(G), or
   2. correctly concludes that mvp(G) > k, or
   3. outputs a graph G0 on O(k 7082 ) vertices and integer `, such that if mvp(G) ≤ k, then
      mvp(G0 ) ≤ 170 · (mvp(G) − `). Furthermore, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given

                                                       69
      G, k, G0 , and a planar modulator S 0 in G0 of size |S 0 | < k+1−3·`
                                                                      3    , outputs a planar modulator
                                   0
      S in G such that |S| ≤ 3 · |S | + 3 · `.

Proof. We will analyze a series of graphs G, G1 , G2 , G3 , G4 = G0 obtained from G by applying
the intermediate reductions. For each reduction we need to prove (a) the forward safeness: that
the existence of a planar modulator in Gi implies the existence of a planar modulator in Gi+1 of
comparable size, and (b) the backward safeness: that when given a planar modulator in Gi+1 within
particular size bound we can lift it to a planar modulator in Gi .
     We begin with Proposition 4.16. We either find a planar modulator in G of size mvp(G) ≤ k, or
correctly conclude that mvp(G) > k, or construct a graph G1 together with a tree decomposition of
width O(k 36 ). In the first two cases we can terminate the algorithm. In the last case we also obtain
an integer k1 ≤ k, such that if mvp(G) ≤ k, then mvp(G1 ) = mvp(G) − (k − k1 ). Furthermore,
there is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given G, k, G1 , and a planar modulator S1 in G1 of size
at most k1 , outputs a planar modulator S in G such that |S| = |S1 | + (k − k1 ).
     Next, we apply Proposition 5.7 to G1 , k1 , and the computed tree decomposition of width
t = O(k 36 ). Again, if we conclude that mvp(G1 ) > k1 then it implies that mvp(G) > k so
we can terminate the algorithm. Otherwise, we compute a graph G2 with an r-strong planar
modulator X2 , where r = 2t + 2, and an integer k2 ≤ k1 , such that mvp(G1 ) ≤ k1 implies
mvp(G2 ) ≤ mvp(G1 ) − (k1 − k2 ) and the fragmentation of X2 in G2 is s = O(k 49 t48 ) = O(k 1777 ).
Furthermore, given a planar modulator S2 in G2 , one can turn it into a planar modulator in G1 of
size at most |S2 | + 3 · (k1 − k2 ) in polynomial time.
     We process the pair (G2 , X2 ) with Proposition 6.21 for the parameter value k2 . We obtain
a new graph G3 , an r-strong modulator X3 in G3 of fragmentation s, and a family of plane
embeddings of G3 hCi for each connected component C ∈ CC(G3 − X3 ), each with a radial diameter
d = O(kr) = O(k 37 ). We know that mvp(G3 ) ≤ mvp(G2 ) and there is a lifting algorithm that can
turn a solution S3 of size at most k2 in G3 into a solution of size at most 3 · |S3 | in G2 .
     The last modification applies Proposition 9.1 to the decomposition computed so far and the
parameter value k2 . We obtain a graph G4 with O(d64 r32 k 8 s2 ) = O(k 7082 ) vertices (37 · 64 + 36 ·
32 + 8 + 1777 · 2 = 7082), such that mvp(G4 ) ≤ 170 · mvp(G3 ) and any planar modulator S4 of size
at most k2 in G4 can be turned, in polynomial time, into a planar modulator of size at most |S4 | in
G3 .
     We set ` = k − k2 . We check the forward safeness: mvp(G) ≤ k ⇒ mvp(G1 ) ≤ k1 ⇒ mvp(G2 ) ≤
k2 = k − ` so mvp(G2 ) ≤ mvp(G) − `. Then mvp(G4 ) ≤ 170 · mvp(G3 ) ≤ 170 · mvp(G2 ) ≤
170 · (mvp(G) − `). Now we check the backward safeness. Suppose that we are given a planar
modulator in S4 in G4 of size less than k+1−3·`
                                              3     ≤ k2 . We follow the chain of the lifting algorithms
backwards from G4 to G to compute the following planar modulators:

   • S3 in G3 of size at most |S4 |,

   • S2 in G2 of size at most 3 · |S3 | < k + 1 − 3 · `, (so |S2 | ≤ k − 3 · ` ≤ k2 ),

   • S1 in G1 of size at most |S2 | + 3 · (k1 − k2 ) ≤ k − 3 · (k − k1 ) ≤ k1 , and finally

   • S in G of size at most |S1 | + (k − k1 ).

We check that |S| ≤ |S2 | + 3 · ` ≤ 3 · |S4 | + 3 · `. The claim follows.

    The main theorem is now a simple corollary from Theorem 9.2. It remains to unroll the definition
of the approximate kernelization and reformulate the guarantees in this language. Recall that for
a graph G, integer k, and S ⊆ V (G), we define Πvp (G, k, S) = min(|S|, k + 1) if S is a planar


                                                    70
modulator in G, and Πvp (G, k, S) = ∞ otherwise. We have that OPTΠvp (G, k) = min(mvp(G), k+1).
Capping the solution cost at k + 1 cannot increase the approximation factor of a solution, that is,
 Πvp (G,k,S)    |S|
OPTΠ (G,k) ≤ mvp(G) . When |S| ≤ k, then no capping occurs and we obtain equality.
      vp

   Polynomial-time approximation As a consequence of Theorem 1.1 we give improved ap-
proximation algorithms for Vertex planarization. This is obtained by pipelining the approximate
kernelization with the following result by Kawarabayashi and Sidiropoulos.
Theorem 9.3 ([KS17]). Vertex planarization admits the following randomized approximation
algorithms on n-vertex graphs.

                                                        71

   We give a general observation on the result of applying ρ(n)-approximation to the outcome of
approximate kernelization.

Lemma 9.4. Let α, c, d, r ∈ N be constants and ρ, f : N → N be functions satisfying:

   1. Vertex planarization admits an α-approximate kernelization of size f , such that f (k) ≤
      c · k d for k ≥ r, and

   2. α · ρ(k 2d ) ≤ k for k ≥ r.

Suppose that Vertex planarization admits a (randomized) ρ(n)-approximation algorithm running
in time T (n) where n is the number of vertices in the input. Then there is a (randomized) algorithm
that, given a graph G and integer `, runs in time O(nr+O(1) ) + T (c2d · `2d ), and either outputs a
planar modulator in G of size at most α · ρ(c2d · `2d ) · mvp(G) or correctly concludes that mvp(G) > `.

Proof. If ` < r we can find a solution of size at most ` in time O(nr+1 ) so suppose that ` ≥ r.
We execute the algorithm from Theorem 1.1 on the instance (G, k) where k = c · `2 and obtain a
new instance (G0 , k 0 ), where G0 has at most n0 = cd+1 · `2d ≤ (c · `)2d vertices. Next, we execute
the ρ(n)-approximation algorithm on G0 and obtain a planar modulator S 0 in G0 of size at most
ρ((c · `)2d ) · mvp(G0 ). We use the solution lifting algorithm to turn S 0 into a planar modulator S in
G so that
                   Πvp (G, k, S)       Πvp (G0 , k 0 , S 0 )         |S 0 |
                                 ≤α·                         ≤ α ·          ≤ α · ρ((c · `)2d ).
                   OPTvp (G, k)        OPTvp (G0 , k 0 )           mvp(G0 )
                                                                  Πvp (G,k,S)       |S|
We will show that when mvp(G) ≤ ` then actually |S| ≤ k and so OPT     vp (G,k)
                                                                                = mvp(G) . As ` ≤ k this
already implies that OPTvp (G, k) = mvp(G). It remains to show that Πvp (G, k, S) ≤ k which implies
Πvp (G, k, S) = |S|. From the estimation above, we have Πvp (G, k, S) ≤ α · ρ((c · `)2d ) · OPTvp (G, k)
but by the assumption on ρ and since r ≤ ` ≤ c · ` and OPTvp (G, k) = mvp(G) ≤ ` this is upper
bounded by (c · `) · ` = k. Hence, if we get |S| > k, we can conclude that mvp(G) > `. Otherwise,
S is an (α · ρ((c · `)2d ))-approximate solution.

   Since approximation factors of the form (log n)O(1) and O(nε ) for sufficiently small ε satisfy the
conditions of Lemma 9.4, we obtain the following consequence.

10      Planarization criteria
In this section we provide the proofs of the planarization criteria stated in Section 3. We first
show how the criterion from Lemma 3.15 can be used to prove that a graph remains planar after
some modification. The following lemma generalizes the case where S is a connected restricted
(v, V (C))-separator which was an argument used in earlier work [JLS14]. This stronger version will
be later needed to prove Lemma 3.21.

Lemma 10.1. Let G be a connected graph, C be a cycle in G, S ⊆ V (G), and v ∈ V (G) \ (V (C) ∪
S). Suppose that |S ∩ V (C)| ≤ 1, G[S] is connected, G[S \ V (C)] is non-empty and connected,
RG (v, S) ∩ V (C) = ∅, and the overlap graph O(G − v, C) is bipartite. Then the overlap graph
O(G, C) is bipartite as well.

Proof. For a C-bridge B we denote by A(B) ⊆ V (C) its set of attachments on C. We say that
B1 , B2 are incomparable if A(B1 ) 6⊆ A(B2 ) and A(B2 ) 6⊆ A(B1 ). We start by showing that inserting
v into G − v cannot merge two incomparable C-bridges B1 , B2 into one C-bridge. Suppose otherwise.
Let b1 and b2 . There exist a (v, b1 )-path P1 in G with internal vertices in V (B1 ) and a (v, b2 )-path P2
with internal vertices in V (B2 ). Suppose first that S ∩ V (C) = ∅. Then S is a connected restricted
(v, V (C))-separator in G and both paths P1 , P2 must cross S. Let P10 , P20 be obtained from P1 , P2
by removing their endpoints; note that they are non-empty. Then V (P10 ) ∪ V (P20 ) ∪ S induces a
connected subgraph of (G − v) − V (C) and contains vertices from both B1 , B2 ; a contradiction with
the assumption that B1 , B2 are distinct C-bridges in G − v.
     Suppose now that S ∩ V (C) = {t}. Assume without loss of generality that b1 6= t, which can
be achieved by swapping B1 and B2 if needed: since they are incomparable, at least one of them
has an attachment point other than t. Since RG (v, S) ∩ V (C) = ∅, the path P1 must intersect
S \ V (C). As G[S \ V (C)] is connected, we obtain S \ V (C) ⊆ V (B1 ) and, since G[S] is connected,
t ∈ A(B1 ). If there exists b2 ∈ A(B2 ) \ {t}, then by the same argument we get S \ V (C) ⊆ V (B2 ),
contradicting that B1 , B2 are distinct C-bridges. Therefore A(B2 ) = {t}, which contradicts that
B1 , B2 are incomparable.
     Using the derived property we conclude the proof. If v is an isolated vertex of G − V (C), then
its insertion to G − v creates a new C-bridge. The attachments of the bridge {v} must belong to
S ∩ V (C) (since RG (v, S) ∩ V (C) = ∅) so the new bridge can have only one attachment. Such a
bridge cannot overlap with any other bridge, so this cannot create any odd cycles in the overlap
graph. Otherwise, inserting v merges some bridges B1 , B2 , . . . , B` (possibly ` = 1) into one and
augments the set of attachments by NG (v) ∩ V (C). There cannot be an incomparable pair in
B1 , B2 , . . . , B` , so there is one C-bridge Bi so that A(Bj ) ⊆ A(Bi ) for j ∈ [`]. If Bj is overlapping
with some bridge B 0 , then also Bi overlaps with B 0 . Similarly as in the previous paragraph, we have
S ∩ V (C) ⊆ A(Bi ) and also NG (v) ∩ V (C) ⊆ S ∩ V (C). Therefore inserting v into G − v cannot
augment the set of attachments of Bi . The graph O(G, C) is thus obtained from O(G − v, C) by
simply removing vertices representing Bj for j 6= i. This also cannot create any odd cycles.

     This is sufficient to prove the “classic” planarity criterion (Lemma 3.16). We present the


                                                    73
following, slightly stronger, lemma, in which one of the separators does not need to be cyclic but
only connected. This directly implies Lemma 3.16 and later will be useful for proving Lemma 3.17.

Lemma 10.2. Let v0 , v3 ∈ V (G) and let S1 , S2 ⊆ V (G) be disjoint nested restricted (v0 , v3 )-
separators in a connected graph G, so that G[S2 ] has a Hamiltonian cycle and G[S1 ] is connected.
If RG [v0 , S2 ] and RG [v3 , S1 ] induce planar graphs, then G is planar.

Proof. First we show that if G − v0 is planar then G is planar as well. Let C be a Hamiltonian
cycle in G[S2 ]. By Lemma 3.15 the overlap graph O(G − v0 , C) is bipartite and for every C-bridge
B in G − v0 the graph B ∪ C is planar. We apply Lemma 10.1 for the set S = S1 which has empty
intersection with V (C), RG (v0 , S) ∩ V (C) = ∅, and the graph G[S] is connected. We obtain that
the overlap graph O(G, C) is bipartite. Let B0 be the C-bridge in G containing v0 . Then B0 ∪ C is
a subgraph of G[RG [v0 , V (C)]] which is planar by assumption. For any other C-bridge B in G, the
graph B ∪ C is a subgraph of G − v0 , hence it is planar as well. Therefore, from Lemma 3.15 we
infer that G is planar.
    Now we give a proof of the lemma by induction on the number of vertices in V (G) \ RG [v3 , S1 ].
If v0 is the only vertex in V (G) \ RG [v3 , S1 ] then G − v0 is planar and the argument above implies
planarity of G. When v0 is an articulation point in G then only one connected component C0 of
G − v0 may be not fully contained in RG [v, S1 ] and by Observation 3.18 it suffices to focus on the
graph G0 = G[V (C0 ) ∪ {v0 }]. The sets S1 , S2 as well as the vertex v3 must be contained in V (G0 ).
If v0 is not an articulation point, we set G0 = G. Let v 0 ∈ V (G) \ RG [v3 , S1 ] be distinct from v0
and contained in G0 − v0 . We have that S1 , S2 are disjoint nested restricted (v 0 , v3 )-separators in a
connected graph G0 − v0 . By induction we obtain that G0 − v0 is planar. Applying the argument
from the first paragraph concludes the proof.

10.1    The new criterion
We move on to proving the new planarity criterion, where instead of 2 cyclic nested separators,
we work with 3 connected nested separators. We start with the following observation about the
behavior of nested separators.

Observation 10.3. If (Si )`i=1 is a sequence of nested vertex-disjoint restricted (v0 , v`+1 )-separators
in a connected graph G such that G[Si ] is connected for all i ∈ [`], then the following holds.

   1. For each i ∈ [` − 1] (resp. i ∈ {2, . . . , `}) we have NG [Si ] ⊆ RG [v0 , Si+1 ] (resp. NG [Si ] ⊆
      RG [v`+1 , Si−1 ]).

   2. For each i ∈ [` − 1] we have RG [v0 , Si ] ⊆ RG [v0 , Si+1 ] and Si ⊆ RG (v0 , Si+1 ).

   The following elementary lemma localizes paths connecting consecutive separators in a nested
sequence.

Lemma 10.4. Let S1 , S2 be two nested vertex-disjoint restricted (v0 , v3 )-separators in a connected
graph G, such that G[Si ] is connected for each i ∈ [2]. If P is an (S1 , S2 )-path in G whose internal
vertices are disjoint from S1 ∪ S2 , then V (P ) ⊆ RG [v0 , S2 ] ∩ RG [v3 , S1 ].

Proof. Let P 0 be the subpath formed by the interior vertices of P . Since each G[Si ] is connected
and G is connected, we have Si ⊆ RG [x, Si ] for each x ∈ {v0 , v3 }. If V (P 0 ) = ∅, then together with
the fact that S1 ⊆ RG [v0 , S2 ] and S2 ⊆ RG [v3 , S1 ] by Observation 10.3, the lemma follows.
    So suppose V (P 0 ) 6= ∅ and orient P 0 so that it starts at a neighbor w1 of S1 and ends in a
neighbor w2 of S2 . By the previous argument, it suffices to show that V (P 0 ) ⊆ RG [v0 , S2 ]∩RG [v3 , S1 ].

                                                     74
By the first point of Observation 10.3 we have w1 ∈ NG [S1 ] ⊆ RG [v0 , S2 ] and since P 0 is by
definition disjoint from S1 ∪ S2 , reachability of the first vertex of P 0 implies reachability of the entire
subpath: V (P 0 ) ⊆ RG [v0 , S2 ]. Conversely, since P 0 ends in w2 ∈ NG [S2 ] ⊆ RG [v3 , S1 ] and P 0 does
not intersect S1 ∪ S2 , path P 0 is contained entirely in RG [v3 , S1 ], which completes the proof.
Lemma 3.17. Let v0 , v4 ∈ V (G) and let S1 , S2 , S3 ⊆ V (G) be disjoint nested restricted (v0 , v4 )-
separators in a connected graph G, so that G[Si ] is connected for each i ∈ [3]. If RG [v0 , S3 ] and
RG [v4 , S1 ] induce planar graphs, then G is planar.
Proof. Since the separators S1 , S2 , S3 are nested, S2 is an (S1 , S3 )-separator. We first argue that
we can assume that S2 is in fact inclusion-wise minimal with respect to simultaneously being
a (restricted) (S1 , S3 ) separator and inducing a connected graph, as follows. If S20 ( S2 is also
a connected (S1 , S3 ) separator, then the sequence S1 , S20 , S3 is also a sequence of nested (v0 , v4 )
separators satisfying the lemma statement, since each (S1 , S3 )-separator is also an (v0 , v4 )-separator
since they are nested. As the graphs induced by RG [v0 , S3 ] and RG [v4 , S1 ] are the same with respect
to this sequence, the triple S1 , S20 , S3 also satisfies the preconditions to the lemma.
    In the remainder may therefore assume that S2 is a (v0 , v4 )-separator which is inclusion-minimal
with respect to being a connected (S1 , S3 )-separator.
    We first deal with an easy case. If |S2 | = 1, then the single vertex x in S2 is an articulation point
that separates v0 from v4 . In this case, it is easy to verify that for each connected component H
of G − x, the set V (H) ∪ {x} is a subset of RG [v0 , S3 ] or RG [v4 , S1 ] and therefore induces a planar
graph by assumption. Hence by Observation 3.18, in this case we are guaranteed that G is planar.
    In the remainder we assume that S2 is not a singleton, and is inclusion-minimal with respect to
being a connected (S1 , S3 )-separator. For the following claim, recall that RG (x, S) denotes the set
of vertices reachable from x in G − S, that is, the vertex set of the connected component of G − S
containing x.
Claim 10.5. G[S2 ] has a Hamiltonian path whose endpoint(s) are adjacent to both RG (v0 , S2 )
and RG (v4 , S2 ).
Proof. Let T be a spanning tree of the connected subgraph G[S2 ]. Since S2 is minimal with
respect to being a connected (S1 , S3 )-separator, each leaf ` of T is adjacent to both the connected
component of G−S2 containing S1 (on vertex set RG (v0 , S2 )) and the connected component of G−S2
containing S3 (on vertex set RG (v4 , S2 )). If T has at most two leaves, this proves the claim. Suppose
for a contradiction that T has at least three leaves `1 , `2 , `3 , so that the set I ⊆ V (T ) = S2 of
internal vertices of T is non-empty.
    Let Q1 be a vertex set containing S1 and three paths, defined as follows. Each leaf `i for i ∈ [3]
has a neighbor wi in RG (v0 , S2 ); add a shortest path from wi to S1 through RG (v0 , S2 ) to Q1 , which
exists since S1 ⊆ RG [v0 , S2 ]. Since G[S1 ] is connected, the graph G[Q1 ] is connected. The paths
added to Q1 are contained in RG [v0 , S2 ] ⊆ RG [v0 , S3 ] by Observation 10.3, and since S1 ⊆ RG [v0 , S3 ]
by connectivity of G and G[S1 ] we have Q1 ⊆ RG [v0 , S3 ].
    The vertex set Q3 is defined similarly: it contains S3 and for each leaf `i it contains a shortest
path Pi0 through RG (v4 , S1 ) from a neighbor wi0 ∈ NG (`i )∩RG (v4 , S1 ) to S3 . By applying Lemma 10.4
to the (S2 , S3 ) path Pi obtained by appending `i ∈ S2 to Pi0 , for each path Pi0 added to Q3 we
have V (Pi0 ) ⊆ RG [v0 , S3 ]. Since S3 ⊆ RG [v0 , S3 ] we have Q3 ⊆ RG [v0 , S3 ].
    The sets defined so far form a minor model of K3,3 : the vertex sets Q1 , Q3 , I are disjoint
and connected and are each adjacent to the three vertices `1 , `2 , `3 ∈       / Q1 ∪ Q3 ∪ I. As we have
shown Q1 , Q3 ⊆ RG [u, S3 ], together with S2 ⊆ RG [u, S3 ] this implies that the entire minor model
is contained in RG [u, S3 ], contradicting the assumption that RG [u, S3 ] induces a planar subgraph
of G.                                                                                                    J

                                                     75
   The following claim shows that there is a cycle in G containing the entire set S2 (which is therefore
a (u, v)-separator) which is contained in the region of the graph ‘in between’ the separators S1
and S2 , and can therefore be shown to be disjoint from S3 .
Claim 10.6. There is a cycle C in G such that S2 ⊆ V (C) ⊆ RG [v0 , S2 ] ∩ RG [v4 , S1 ].

Proof. Let H be a Hamiltonian path of G[S2 ] whose two endpoints `1 , `2 are adjacent to both RG (v0 , S2 )
and RG (v4 , S2 ), as guaranteed by Claim 10.5.
    For each i ∈ [2], let Pi be an (S1 , `i )-path in G whose internal vertices are disjoint from S1 ∪ S2 .
Such a path exists since `i has a neighbor in the connected set RG (v0 , S2 ) ⊇ S1 . By Lemma 10.4,
we therefore have V (Pi ) ⊆ (RG [v0 , S2 ] ∩ RG [v3 , S1 ]). Since G[S1 ] is connected, the subgraph G[S2 ∪
V (P1 ) ∪ V (P2 )] is connected and contains a simple (`1 , `2 )-path P with at least one internal
vertex, such that the interior of P is disjoint from S2 . Then V (P ) ⊆ S2 ∪ V (P1 ) ∪ V (P2 ) ⊆
RG [v0 , S3 ] ∩ RG [v4 , S1 ]. Hence P together with the Hamiltonian (`1 , `2 )-path T spanning S2 forms
a cycle C as desired, completing the proof.                                                               J

    Fix a cycle C as guaranteed by the previous claim. Note that V (C)∩S3 = ∅ since S3 ∩RG [v0 , S2 ] =
∅ by the nesting property of the separators. Similarly we infer that v0 6∈ V (C). Furthermore, V (C) is
a (v0 , S3 )-separator because S2 ⊆ V (C) and S3 is a (V (C), v4 )-separators because V (C) is contained
in the same component of G − S3 as S2 . Hence, (V (C), S3 ) are disjoint nested (v0 , v4 )-separators
which satisfy the prerequisites of Lemma 10.2 (modulo ordering) and this concludes the proof.

10.2    Irrelevant vertices
Finally, we show how the planarity criteria give sufficient conditions for a vertex/edge to be
k-irrelevant.

Lemma 3.19. Let G be a connected graph, k ∈ N, v ∈ V (G), and S1 , S2 , . . . , Sk+3 ⊆ V (G) \ {v}
be disjoint, connected, nested (with respect to v), and v-planarizing. Then v is k-irrelevant in G
and every edge incident to v is k-irrelevant in G.

Proof. Let X ⊆ V (G) \ {v} be any planar modulator in G − v of size at most k; we have that
(G − v) − X is planar. We are going to show that G − X is planar as well. There must exist indices
i1 < i2 < i3 so that Sij ∩ X = ∅ for j ∈ [3]. The sets Si1 , Si2 , Si3 form a nested sequence (with
respect to v) in the graph G − X. They are v-planarizing and induce connected subgraphs of G,
so the same holds in G − X. Consider the connected component of v in G − X; let us refer to
it as Gv . If V (Gv ) ⊆ RG−X [v, Si3 ] then, since Si3 planarizes v, this component is planar. Other
connected components of G − X are subgraphs of (G − v) − X so the entire graph G − X is planar.
Otherwise there exists a vertex u ∈ V (Gv ) \ RG−X [v, Si3 ]. Now the sets Si1 , Si2 , Si3 are nested
(v, u)-separators. Note that RG−X [u, Si1 ] ⊆ V (G) \ {v} so it induces a planar subgraph of G − X.
We apply Lemma 3.17 to infer that Gv is planar and thus G − X is planar. This concludes the
proof that v is k-irrelevant. If e is an edge incident to v, then any solution to G \ e is also a solution
to G − v, so the edge case reduces to the vertex case.

Lemma 3.21. Let G be a connected graph containing v ∈ V (G), let k ∈ N and let (S1 , S2 , . . . , Sk+3 )
be a pseudo-nested sequence of cycles with respect to v, such that each set Si is v-planarizing. Then
v is k-irrelevant in G and every edge incident to v is k-irrelevant in G.

Proof. Let t ∈ V (G), t 6= v, be the common intersection of every pair of sets Si , Sj , 1 ≤ i < j ≤ k + 3.
Let X ⊆ V (G) \ {v} be any planar modulator in G − v of size at most k; we have that (G − v) − X


                                                    76
is planar. We are going to show that G − X is planar as well. We consider two cases depending on
whether t ∈ S or not.
    Suppose first that t ∈ X and let Xt = X \ {t}. Then Xt is a planar modulator in G − t of size
at most k − 1. Observe that {Si \ {t} | i ∈ [k + 3]} are disjoint and form a (standard) nested (with
respect to v) sequence in G − t. They also induce connected subgraphs of G − t as removal of a single
vertex from a cycle cannot disconnect it. Analogously as in the proof of Lemma 3.19, we consider
indices i1 < i2 < i3 so that Sij ∩ Xt = ∅ for j ∈ [3]. The sequence (Si1 \ {t}, Si2 \ {t}, Si3 \ {t}) is
nested and v-planarizing in (G − t) − Xt . From the assumption that (G − v) − X = ((G − t) − v) − Xt
is planar and by Lemma 3.17 we get that (G − t) − Xt = G − X is planar.
    Suppose now that t 6∈ S. Again by a counting argument, we can pick i, j ∈ [k + 3], i < j such
that Si ∩ X = Sj ∩ X = ∅. We have that RG−X (v, Si ) ∩ Sj = ∅ and |Si ∩ Sj | = 1. Furthermore G[Si ]
and G[Si \ Sj ] are connected. Let C be the Hamiltonian cycle in G[Sj ]. We shall use the criterion
from Lemma 3.15 directly. The graph (G − v) − X is planar so the overlap graph O((G − v) − X, C)
is bipartite and for each C-bridge B we have that B ∪ C is planar. We apply Lemma 10.1 to the
graph G − X, cycle C, and the set Si to infer that the overlap graph O(G − X, C) is also bipartite.
Let Bv be the C-bridge in G − X which contains v. Then Bv ∪ C is subgraph of G[RG [v, Sj ]] which
is planar by the assumption. Since the other C-bridges are unaffected, Lemma 3.15 implies that
G − X is planar.
    This concludes the proof that v is k-irrelevant. Again, if e is an edge incident to v, then any
solution to G \ e is also a solution to G − v, so the edge case reduces to the vertex case.


11     Conclusion
We presented the first constant-factor approximate kernelization for Vertex planarization.
While several of our reduction steps are lossy and cannot directly be used in an exact kerneliza-
tion, we believe the insights from our work will be important towards settling whether Vertex
planarization admits a polynomial-size exact kernelization. In turn, this will shed further light
on the algorithmic complexity of F-minor free deletion for families F that do not contain any
planar graphs.
    At the heart of our lossy kernelization is the subroutine to compute a bounded-size preserver
which contains a good approximate solution. Through several reduction steps, we showed that it
suffices to perform this computation on planar subgraphs which can be embedded in the plane
such that the boundary vertices by which they communicate with the rest of the graph all lie on
the outer face and are bounded in number. Whether an exact preserver of polynomial size exists
for such subgraphs, and whether it can be computed efficiently, is an important open question for
future work. To emphasize the importance of this question, we restate it here explicitly:

Open problem 11.1. Determine whether there exists a polynomial f : N → N such that the following
holds. Every planar k-boundaried graph H that has an embedding with all boundary vertices on the
outer face, contains a set A of f (k) vertices such that for any k-boundaried graph G, there is an
optimal solution S to the Vertex planarization problem on H ⊕ G satisfying S ∩ V (H) ⊆ A.

   In hindsight, the insight that the task of (approximately) kernelizing Vertex planarization
can be reduced to analyzing planar subgraphs interacting with the rest of the graph through their
outer face, and therefore that the problem is amenable to techniques typically applied only for
problems whose input graph is planar, is one of the conceptual take-away messages of this work.
Driven by this realization, we believe that the answer to Problem 11.1 will be the key to settling
the kernelization complexity of Vertex planarization.


                                                  77
    A second question is whether every F-minor free deletion problem admits a constant-factor
approximate kernelization. Although a number of steps in our algorithm can be generalized to
graphs excluding any fixed minor, we heavily depend on the properties of plane graphs when
decomposing the locally planar components. It is plausible that these arguments may be generalized
to graph families (near-)embeddable on more complex surfaces. A technically daunting but possibly
viable challenge is therefore to try using the Robertson-Seymour decomposition [KTW20, RS03] of
graphs excluding a fixed minor H to develop an analog of the solution preservers framework on
H-minor-free subgraphs for arbitrary H.
